#366. T-Mobile illegally imposed 50GB cap on low-income home Internet plan, NY says

Law requires 25Mbps home Internet. T-Mobile lowers speed to 600Kbps after 50GB cap.

T-Mobile violated a New York state law by imposing a 50GB-per-month data cap and reducing speeds to less than a megabit per second on home Internet plans it is required to offer people with low incomes, New York government officials alleged.

T-Mobile agreed to a settlement requiring it to scrap the data and speed limits, to offer the required low-income plans to more parts of the state, and to make it easier for customers to find information about the plans. The settlement requires T-Mobile to eliminate the speed limits and expand availability of the plans within 120 days.

The alleged legal violation and the settlement are related to T-Mobile’s wireless home Internet service, not the company’s mobile plans. Governor Kathy Hochul and New York Attorney General Letitia James announced the settlement on Monday, saying that a state investigation found T-Mobile violated the New York Affordable Broadband Act (ABA).

The state law doesn’t specifically mention data caps, but it requires home Internet providers with at least 20,000 customers to offer plans at specific prices and minimum speeds to people who meet income-eligibility requirements. T-Mobile’s policy of reducing speeds after customers exceed a monthly data cap violates the law, New York officials said.

“An investigation by the Office of the Attorney General (OAG) found T-Mobile’s offerings did not meet the ABA’s requirements, which mandate that home Internet providers offer high-speed broadband to income-eligible households for $15 per month at speeds of at least 25 Mbps, or $20 per month for speeds of at least 200 Mbps,” the press release said.

New York says in the settlement with T-Mobile that “after the data cap is reached, the customer’s maximum download speed is reduced from 25 Mbps to 600 Kbps.” New York’s press release said “a typical customer could hit the data cap after about four days of usage.”

Data caps and speed limits aren’t the only restrictions. T-Mobile also “only offers ABA Internet plans to customers in homes that are eligible for 5G home Internet,” preventing residents in 4G-only areas from accessing the plans, the settlement says. The company also prevented search engines from indexing the webpage that provides detailed information about eligibility for and enrollment in ABA plans, it says.

“T-Mobile did not fully comply with the New York Affordable Broadband Act,” New York said in the settlement.

T-Mobile’s ABA webpage confirms that customers are provided only 50GB per month and that speeds after using the allotment are merely “up to 600 Kbps.”

Although the settlement includes a list of the New York AG’s findings, T-Mobile did not admit to any violation. “Respondent neither admits nor denies the OAG’s Finding in paragraph 1-14 above,” the document says.

New York started enforcing the Affordable Broadband Act in January 2025 after winning a nearly four-year court battle against broadband industry lobby groups that tried to kill the law. The groups that sued New York in a failed effort to block the law included CTIA, the wireless industry association that represents T-Mobile and other carriers. The law finally took effect a month after the Supreme Court rejected the broadband industry’s challenge.

When contacted by Ars, T-Mobile did not explain why it imposed the restrictions on low-income plans.

“We have a long history of helping customers in New York stay connected through low-cost broadband, and we’re updating our plan to meet the state’s requirements while maintaining the same low price,” T-Mobile said in a statement. “We’ve reached an agreement with the New York attorney general that builds on that commitment, provides greater clarity around our offering and makes the plan available to more eligible customers.”

The settlement with New York applies to T-Mobile’s fixed wireless Internet plans, which can be used for home Internet service as an alternative to wired broadband. The fixed wireless Internet relies on T-Mobile’s cellular network but provides access at a single location, delivering wireless signals to receivers installed at customer homes.

It took almost 18 months for New York to investigate T-Mobile’s compliance and reach a settlement with the company. “In June 2025, the New York Department of Public Service contacted the OAG and relayed concerns about certain features of the plan that T-Mobile was offering to customers eligible for reduced-price Internet service under the ABA,” the settlement said.

It seems that ISPs have generally offered the required plans, though cable company Optimum failed to comply until Ars made some inquiries on behalf of a customer who could not obtain the plan he was entitled to shortly after the law took effect. ISPs knew about the law long in advance due to the multiyear court case that they initiated, but Optimum wasn’t ready to comply with the requirement and rejected the low-income man’s request twice.

New York officials said the AG’s office “found that T-Mobile was only offering ABA plans in areas where it had deployed 5G wireless service, even though it offered full-price Internet plans that could deliver 25 Mbps download speeds in areas where it had 4G service. In addition, T-Mobile specifically coded its website to hide its ABA plan information from search engines, making it difficult for New Yorkers searching for low-priced Internet to find their plans.”

The settlement requires T-Mobile to stop intentionally limiting download speeds, and says the “temporary reduction of download speeds due solely to network congestion shall not constitute a violation of this Assurance.” There’s also an exception in the state law, which says ISPs shall provide 25Mbps “subject to exceptions adopted by the Public Service Commission where such download speed is not reasonably practicable.”

Extending the low-cost service to 4G areas, T-Mobile agreed to provide the low-income plans “at every address at which Respondent offers residential fixed wireless Internet service.” Speeds lower than 25 Mbps would not violate the settlement if T-Mobile “would be unable to provide a minimum download speed of 25 megabits per second to a residential fixed wireless Internet service customer enrolled in a non-ABA Plan at the same address.”

The document says that “T-Mobile offers 5G home Internet to roughly 40 percent of homes in rural areas of upstate New York and 4G (LTE) home Internet to the remainder of homes in the regions where T-Mobile offers fixed wireless service.”

To make its ABA webpage more accessible, the settlement says T-Mobile “shall not include a noindex tag on the webpage, or otherwise prevent search engines from indexing the webpage or presenting a link to the webpage in response to search engine queries.” T-Mobile must also prominently link to the ABA webpage on the part of its website that lists or describes its standard home Internet deals.

New York could sue T-Mobile if it violates the settlement. The ABA’s enforcement mechanism requires the attorney general to sue a broadband provider on behalf of residents. Such lawsuits can recover restitution and civil penalties of up to $1,000 per violation.

ISPs that want to raise another challenge to New York’s law could get help from the Trump administration, which asserts that states must not enforce price regulations or net neutrality laws against any ISP that obtains grants from the $42 billion Broadband Equity, Access, and Deployment (BEAD) program. The grants have not been distributed yet, and California argues that the Trump administration does not have authority to preempt such laws.

In addition to this week’s settlement, New York struck a deal with T-Mobile last month over the carrier violating its promise to never raise prices on certain mobile plans that came with a lifetime price guarantee.

A state investigation “found that from 2017 to 2019, T-Mobile advertised ‘Price Guarantee Plans’ to its 4G customers, promising to ‘never change the price you pay,’” the New York attorney general’s office said on October 20. “However, in 2024, T-Mobile changed course and began raising the price of these plans by approximately $5 per phone line. New Yorkers enrolled in approximately 252,957 of these Price Guarantee Plans saw their phone bills increase.”

T-Mobile agreed to give affected customers several reimbursement options and a price lock until May 2029. The attorney general’s office estimated that the agreement would provide up to $38 million in value to customers affected by the price hikes. T-Mobile also agreed to pay the state a fine of $98,834.75.

T-Mobile provided a statement on the price-hike settlement.

“The $38 million cited in the press release is an estimate of potential benefits that a group of long-standing T-Mobile customers may choose to take advantage of, not the amount in refunds or payments,” T-Mobile said. “The agreement provides for approximately $98,000 paid to the state and a range of optional customer benefits. We’re glad to have this matter resolved and remain focused on delivering value to our customers.”

#364. Cash App founder's killer gets 16 years to life in prison

Bob Lee died from multiple stab wounds in 2023.

Two years after his conviction for second-degree murder, Nima Momeni, the tech consultant found guilty of killing Cash App founder Bob Lee, has been sentenced. San Francisco Superior Court Judge Alexandra Gordon handed him to 16 years to life in prison.

Lee was instrumental in the development of Cash App while serving as chief technology officer at Square. He was also an engineer at Google from 2004 to 2010 and was involved in the development of Android. Lee was later the chief product officer at crypto payments company MobileCoin and helped the World Health Organization launch its mobile app during the COVID-19 pandemic.

In April 2023, Lee was found bleeding in a doorway on an empty downtown San Francisco street after being stabbed in the hip and heart. He managed to call 911 but died after being taken to a hospital.

Following the killing but before an arrest was made, some of Big Tech's loudest voices were quick to frame it in terms that supported their biases. "Many people I know have been severely assaulted" in San Francisco, Elon Musk tweeted, suggesting that "even if attackers are caught, they are often released immediately." Investor Jason Calacanis chimed in, calling those running the city "incompetent lunatics," while claiming the city was devolving into "utter chaos." (San Fransisco has a comparatively low crime rate, and has seen its homicide rate fall over the last four years.)

As it happened, this had nothing to do with governmental incompetence or random acts of violence. Lee was involved with Momeni's sister and had been partying with the two on the night of the murder. Prosecutors said Momeni killed Lee after learning that the Cash App founder had introduced his sister to drug dealers who abused her. Momeni's team claimed he acted in self-defense after Lee lunged at him with a knife. Lee was found to have cocaine, ketamine and alcohol in his system.

The judge rejected Momeni's request for a new trial. His lawyers say they plan to appeal.

#361. Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO: Successor, reactions, live updates

Jensen Huang officially resigned as Nvidia CEO on Thursday morning, a seismic event in the tech industry.

Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies in the world, with a nearly $4 trillion valuation, and Huang was the company's original CEO and co-founder. The company's board of directors shared a CEO transition plan on its website, saying Huang has also resigned as president and as a member of the board, effective immediately.

The company's CFO, Colette Kress, will serve as interim CEO, while also maintaining her CFO responsibilities. The board has begun searching for a new CEO, looking both inside and outside the company.

The tech industry has been closely watching Nvidia and its stock ever since news first broke by the New York Times that Huang had not one, but two affairs, including one with an accused Chinese spy. Nvidia chips provide much of the compute that powers the fast-growing AI industry, and so Huang's alleged entanglement with a possible Chinese spy wasn't just a typical executive PR scandal, but a national security issue.

The story is still developing, so keep checking back at Mashable for the latest Nvidia news and live updates.

Last Friday, New York Times and Financial Times reported dropped a bomb on the tech industry. The publications reported that Huang had an affair with a woman the U.S. intelligence community believed to be a Chinese spy. China disputed these accusations, of course, but the Times reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio personally wrote a memo alerting President Donald Trump to the situation in the spring.

On Nov. 8, Huang posted a personal message to the Nvidia website, admitting to and apologizing for the affair.

"I owe my family and all of you an apology," Huang wrote. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

It was unclear whether Huang would be able to hold on to his position as CEO, and the Nvidia board of directors announced it had opened an investigation into the incident.

But on Wednesday, news of a second affair may have sealed Huang's fate. The New York Times reported that Huang had another extramarital affair with another Nvidia executive who reported to him. The Times identified that executive as Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare.

#359. Review: Apple's M6 iMac is pricey, but it's still a good choice for a clean desk

Apple’s M6 keeps the iMac current without shaking things up much.

The Apple Silicon iMac got its fourth refresh this month.

Every other Apple Silicon Mac has found a way to move forward over the last half-decade—we’ve gotten a more Pro design for the MacBook Pro, a mini-er design for the Mac mini, and a Mac Studio that has finally made Apple admit it doesn’t want to make the Mac Pro anymore.

But the Apple Silicon iMac has always been more interested in looking backward. It’s the thin, screen-on-a-stand iMac design by way of the original Bondi Blue G3 iMac. It’s a clean and pretty desktop computer, one I see all the time in small offices and on TV shows that depict small offices. We’re long past the days when it was Apple’s primary workstation computer.

So I wanted to write about this new iMac from the perspective of that entry-level iMac buyer, who chooses the iMac because it’s simple and then uses it until it breaks. What does a new iMac get you if you’re stepping up from the M1 model or comparing it against an older refurbished M4 model? Without any real external updates, what makes this iMac iteration interesting?

If you haven’t used an Apple Silicon-era iMac before, here’s a brief primer.

The new iMac is much thinner than the older aluminum Intel models. Its front is mostly one big 24-inch screen, surrounded on all sides by a relatively thick white bezel (I still wish black were at least an option, but Apple apparently doesn’t agree).

White bezel aside, it’s by far Apple’s most colorful computer. The iMacs ship with color-matched fronts, backs, stands, keyboards, mice, trackpads, and cables. Apple has tweaked the color offerings and changed the tints of each specific color a few times over the years, but the M6 iMac uses the same six color tints (plus silver) that the M4 iMac did.

The basic $1,499 iMac is essentially a MacBook Air in terms of ports—it has two Thunderbolt ports on the back and a headphone jack on the left side. Choosing this model also locks you into a lesser version of the Apple M6 with 10 CPU cores and 10 GPU cores instead of 12 of each, a maximum of 24GB of RAM, a minimum of 256GB of storage, and a maximum of 1TB. Its default keyboard doesn’t have Touch ID support, and the only way to get it is to buy the version with the built-in number pad, an $80 upsell.

The $1,699 version is the one to get if you still intend to use the iMac as a workstation. It includes the full-speed version of the M6 with all its CPU and GPU cores intact (though there are performance caveats here we’ll talk about later), plus options for 32GB of RAM and up to 2TB of storage. This version also includes four Thunderbolt ports on the back for connecting additional accessories. Both available keyboards on this version have Touch ID.

A 2.5 gigabit Ethernet port can be added to any iMac for $30. This port is located on the desktop’s power brick rather than the iMac itself, an elegant solution that keeps even a wired Internet connection hidden beneath your desk with the other cables. All iMacs also offer a VESA mount adapter option in place of the default stand at no extra charge.

I still really like the effect created by the color-matched keyboard, mouse/trackpad, and cables—even the white bezel complements the white keycaps and mouse. Just know you’re paying quite a lot for how the desktop looks. The $899 Mac mini is $800 cheaper than the iMac with the same chip, the same amount of RAM, and the same amount of storage. Sure, it would cost something to add a nice-looking 4K monitor, but not $800, even if you go for extra frills like a high refresh rate.

It’s fine that the iMac upcharges you for style and convenience. That’s the point of the computer. Just make sure that’s what you want to pay for.

Here are the basic specs for every entry-level iMac Apple has shipped, going all the way back to the last of the Intel models.

The iMac’s evolution has been slow and gradual during the Apple Silicon era. The computer has never gotten a base storage upgrade, and the base M4 and M6 models still only support a single external display (the upgraded models support two external displays, finally surpassing the old Intel model).

If you have any kind of Apple Silicon iMac and you’re happy with how it’s performing and you’re not hankering for more RAM or more displays, I don’t think the M6 model really adds anything worth upgrading for. It’s definitely faster, but even six years on, the Apple M1 remains perfectly serviceable for daily use, especially when it’s attached to 16GB of RAM.

And if you’re in the market for an iMac now, I think the M4 model still gets you the best bang for your buck if you can find one refurbished or on sale. That one still gets you a minimum of 16GB of RAM, Thunderbolt in all four ports for the four-port model, the 32GB RAM option, the nano-texture display option, and support for multiple external displays (again, in the four-port model).

The M6 iMac builds on that, and it comes with real performance benefits (more on that shortly). But the brightest dividing line in the Apple Silicon iMac model history is between the M3 and M4 model, not the M4 and M6 model. And that’s true despite the two-generation silicon upgrade.

A quick recap on the M6: a total of 12 CPU cores (two super cores, four performance cores, six efficiency cores), either 10 or 12 GPU cores, 16/24/32GB RAM options, and a doubled Neural Engine NPU with 32 cores instead of 16. For the iMac, the M6 also introduces some of the architectural tweaks introduced with the Apple M5, including the addition of neural accelerator hardware to the GPU cores. This boosts the speed of some kinds of local AI workloads compared to the M4, including MetalFX upscaling and frame generation in games.

The M6 in the Mac mini and the M6 in the 14-inch MacBook Pro both perform similarly within a roughly 28 W sustained power envelope. The version with 16GB of RAM will perform slightly worse in games and other workloads sensitive to memory bandwidth—the 16GB M6 offers 153 GB/s of memory bandwidth, and the 24GB and 32GB versions offer 170 GB/s. But that’s a pretty minor difference.

The iMac is slower than either of those two Macs, particularly in multi-core CPU benchmarks and graphics benchmarks, and the HandBrake video encoding test shows the likely culprit. The iMac’s M6 consumes an average of 18 W of power under sustained heavy load. It won’t throttle its speeds as aggressively as a MacBook Air because the iMac still includes an active cooling fan, but the reduced power envelope does mean that the Mac mini or MacBook Pro are 5 to 8 percent faster than the iMac in heavy multi-core CPU tasks and about 8 to 12 percent faster in graphics benchmarks.

One benefit of the smaller power envelope is that the iMac’s chip is even more power-efficient than the regular M6 and the iMac’s version of the M4, using much less power to do the same amount of work.

That still makes it a solid upgrade over the M4. The iMac’s M6 is 15 to 20 percent faster in our single-core CPU benchmarks, between 20 and 30 percent faster in multi-core CPU benchmarks, and between 20 and 40 percent faster in graphics benchmarks (the Blender GPU benchmarks do even better—they seem to like the neural accelerators in the M5 and M6 GPUs). Especially with 24GB or 32GB of RAM, it’s perfectly capable of being the heart of a multi-monitor workstation setup or a light photo/video editing rig.

The M6 iMac changes almost nothing about who the iMac is for, its position in Apple’s lineup, or what it’s technically capable of. It’s a box-ticking upgrade, and the box being ticked is “keep the insides up to date.”

That means today’s iMac is best for the same kinds of computer users it was good for in 1998: people who don’t want a laptop, people who need a friendly and approachable kiosk or office computer, and people who just want something pretty that doesn’t require a lot of fussing with screens or cables.

That’s the main thing I miss about being an iMac user. I like how my current Mac Studio desktop works, and I need flexibility and complexity in my specific setup to get the Mac and a gaming PC connected to all the same stuff. But despite my best efforts, the cable situation has always been a barely managed mess.

If someone you know has a cable management problem and doesn’t need the complexity, congratulations, you’ve found someone the iMac is for. I just wish Apple would use these kinds of pretty, saturated colors in a laptop or desktop that meets my needs, you know?

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#358. How many SpaceX Starship rocket tests have failed?

In late September 2026, SpaceX's Starship entered orbit for the first time, deploying 26 Starlink satellites in what was heralded as a major step for SpaceX's cosmic ambitions. However, the celebration was cut short when a failed engine forced the mission to end shortly after reaching orbit. The malfunction prevented the intended six-revolution trek through Earth's orbit, falling well short of its 10-hour goal.

This result is typical for Starship, as the company's "fail fast, learn fast" mindset recasts the program's failures — anywhere between four and 11 of 14 total launches, depending on how generous you are — as opportunities for growth. This has been true since its first test flight in April 2023, which exploded 65 miles short of its intended climb into Earth's atmosphere. As Musk tweeted after the fiery test, "Congrats @SpaceX team on an exciting test launch of Starship! Learned a lot for next test launch."

Given SpaceX's success to date, it is hard to argue with the company's logic. According to analytics firm BryceTech, SpaceX launched 85 percent of all spacecraft in 2025, including over half the world's orbital launches. To boot, Starlink's 9,800 active satellites currently account for over half of the world's total, according to satellite tracker Orbital Radar.

The largest and most powerful rocket ever assembled, Starship has a cargo capacity five times larger than SpaceX's Falcon 9 rocket. This makes it essential for SpaceX's grander ambitions, including its million-satellite constellation, NASA's lunar missions and Musk's Mars colony dream. With another launch set for November 13, 2026, stakeholders will soon see whether the September launch represented progress or foreshadowed further additions to SpaceX's growing list of frustrations.

Gauging the success of the Starship project is difficult. Although some missions may fit within a binary success-failure matrix, most land somewhere in between. And while it has yet to deliver a fully-reusable rocket capable of missions to "Mars and beyond," progress was always going to be incremental. As such, its only fair to judge each mission against its own standards of progress.

Starship's start was turbulent. After its initial test combusted within minutes, its second flight saw the first-stage booster separate before the booster and vessel met fiery ends. Five months later, Starship's third test flight completed many of its objectives, reaching an altitude of 145 miles before disintegrating on its return. SpaceX then had two back-to-back successful launches, culminating in its launch tower successfully catching Starship's first-stage booster.

Unfortunately, another troubled period followed. Test six saw SpaceX call off its lauded mechanical arm before crashing the Super Heavy booster into the Gulf of Mexico. Two months later, Starship 7 exploded over the Bahamas just eight minutes after takeoff, sparking investigations by both the FAA and the Turks and Caicos government. The FAA launched another investigation after Starship's eighth test exploded in space, grounding planes across Florida. Starship 9 achieved minor improvements before spinning out of control, forcing SpaceX to cancel its planned deployment of 8 mock satellites.

Starship followed the disastrous run with a pair of its most successful tests. In August 2025, Starship released its first cargo of Starlink satellites. Two months later, SpaceX successfully launched and landed its 11th Starship rocket, fulfilling all major objectives.

Starship's twelfth test launched its upgraded Starship V3 rocket. While it accomplished several objectives, engine failures caused it to crash into the Gulf, triggering yet another FAA investigation. In July 2026, Starship 13 successfully deployed 20 of Starlink's new high-powered satellites before its first-stage booster failed to stick the landing. Considering its scattershot history, Starship 14's semi-successful run is par for the course.

Judging each mission against its stated objectives, most Starship launches have failed to meet expectations. SpaceX, however, would likely claim that its willingness to learn from mistakes puts its true failure rate closer to zero. The better question is whether the program is learning quickly enough to keep pace with its own meteoric ambitions.

If one takes Musk's proclamations as a measuring stick, Starship has underdelivered. In 2025, Musk predicted there was a 50 percent chance Starship would land on Mars in 2026. And with the once-every-26-months Mars launch window closing this November, you can guess which side of the coin SpaceX landed on. Of course, Musk is notorious for overpromising, so this is business as usual at Starbase.

Earlier this year, NASA announced that the program had fallen behind schedule by at least two years since the Agency selected SpaceX's rocket to carry its Artemis moon lander in 2021. With China aiming to reach the moon by decade's end, the pressure is mounting. NASA hopes to return to the moon in 2028; to do so, Starship must meet the Artemis III deadline in summer 2027.

Beyond being Musk's conduit to Mars, SpaceX's most ambitious projects depend on Starship to slash costs. SpaceX's proposed 1 million-satellite datacenter constellation Starmind, for example, is impossible without a high-capacity reusable rocket. The November 13 test should provide another data point to judge SpaceX's progress, especially given its ambitions. In a Q3 earnings call, Musk stated that SpaceX will attempt to catch both the rocket and its booster, making it the  "first time that an orbital launch vehicle has been fully recovered." During the event, Musk said the mission was "more likely than not" to succeed. Starship's history may say otherwise.

#357. Everything Trump has said about Jensen Huangs Nvidia saga

The company that makes the chips behind much of the AI we use every day is suddenly looking for a new CEO.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded Nvidia and ran it for 33 years, resigned Thursday after reports of two extramarital affairs: one was reportedly with a Nvidia executive who worked for him named Kimberly Powell, the other was with Alice Huang (no relation), a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for Beijing.

Before all this, Trump and Huang knew each other pretty well. Huang was already one of Trump's tech advisers, and Nvidia had been asking the administration to let it sell more AI chips in China. When Trump invited Huang to Beijing at the last minute in May, the executive hopped aboard Air Force One during a fuel stop in Alaska.

When the allegations involving Alice Huang came up at Mar-a-Lago on Oct. 31, reporters asked whether Trump would keep Huang on as a tech adviser. Trump said he'd like to. "I’ve always found Jensen Huang to be very above board," he told reporters at his Florida resort on Halloween.

A little over a week later, on Nov. 8, reporters asked Trump again about Jensen Huang's future, this time at Nvidia. Earlier that day, Huang had apologized to employees in a bombshell email admitting he'd been unfaithful to his wife. Nvidia opened an investigation into whether he'd been targeted by foreign intelligence after reports of an affair with suspected spy Alice Huang. The board said at the time it had no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security had been compromised. Trump agreed, called Huang "an honorable guy." Did he think Huang should still run Nvidia? "I think so, oh yeah, sure."

Trump said he hadn't spoken to Huang about the allegations when he made those remarks. When he spoke to reporters again on Monday, Nov. 9, he said he'd asked Huang whether he knew about Alice Huang's alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang told the president he had no idea, according to Trump. "I really believe him," the president said. That left a question about what Trump himself knew. On Monday, Nov. 9, a reporter asked whether he'd heard about the allegations when he and Huang flew to Beijing in May. "No, I didn’t hear about it then," Trump said. The Washington Post reported on Thursday, Nov. 12, that Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump about the alleged relationship aboard Air Force One on May 12, before Huang boarded in Alaska.

As new information from the New York Times and Reuters started coming out with reports that Huang had also had an affair with an Nvidia executive who reported to him, it became a little more difficult to maintain trust in the Nvidia boss — Thursday morning, Nvidia announced that Huang had resigned as CEO and stepped down from the company's board.

Trump wasted little time pitching a replacement only a few hours later. He wrote on Truth Social:

NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend! President DONALD J. TRUMP

For now, Nvidia's finance chief, Colette Kress, is running the company while the board looks for a permanent CEO.

As for Cook, he still has a job at Apple. He stayed on as executive chairman after stepping down as CEO in September, and neither he nor Apple immediately responded when Reuters asked about Trump's suggestion.

#356. Kenyan Domestic Worker Sentenced to 100 Lashes in Saudi Arabia

The kingdom’s promised reforms don’t always match the realities of its justice system. Unmarried women, particularly foreign workers, face unique perils.

Abdi Latif Dahir reported from Beirut, Lebanon, and Vivian Nereim from Riyadh, Saudi Arabia.

A Saudi court has sentenced a domestic worker from Kenya to 100 lashes for giving birth outside marriage.

The ruling, issued last month, shows the limits of a decade of social and legal reforms in a kingdom where women can be jailed and punished for “illegal pregnancy.” The sentence is a harsh turn in what has been a terrifying ordeal for a mother who has tried for more than a year to return to Kenya with her child but has been blocked at every turn.

The mother, Esther Wanja Muthoni, is one of thousands of East African domestic workers who were recruited to work as housekeepers and nannies in Saudi Arabia. A New York Times investigation last year showed how Ms. Muthoni and others wind up trapped in a bureaucratic maze if they become pregnant.

Ms. Muthoni and her 1-year-old son, Abudy, are victims of an exploitative industry that recruits African women to Saudi Arabia in a labor pipeline from which top Kenyan government officials profit. Hundreds of Kenyan women have been killed, and reports of rapes and beatings are common.

For women who become pregnant, giving birth brings added punishment. The children of unwed mothers are routinely deprived of birth certificates in Saudi Arabia. Kenya’s diplomats, who could issue birth certificates and help mothers and children get home, instead frequently ignore them, insult them and solicit bribes or sex.

#354. Hundreds of streaming services and nothing to watch

Most viewers wish there was a TV Guide for streaming.

Bruce Springsteen famously said those words during a concert and later in a song in the 1990s about the number of TV channels seemingly growing disproportionately to the availability of enjoyable content.

Within a few years, there would be hundreds of TV channels and still, some would argue in hyperbolic frustration, “nothing to watch.”

The advent of streaming services was poised as a potential solution to this problem. It granted viewers easy, on-demand access to thousands of movies and shows, which, theoretically, could ensure that viewers could always find something to watch.

According to a survey from Nielsen’s Gracenote, though, that’s not exactly the case today.

Gracenote, a Nielsen-owned content data business, surveyed 3,000 video streaming users in the US, Brazil, France, Germany, Mexico, and the UK who were at least 18 years old for its “2026 State of Play: Amid fragmentation frustrations, UX is the future of streaming” report. It found that many struggle to find something to stream. However, this is not necessarily because there’s nothing good on, but rather because they find it difficult to home in on good content on streaming services.

For example, over 51 percent of respondents said that “the abundance of content and [streaming] services is making it harder to find something to watch.”

Further, 19.2 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either “always” or “often” don’t know what to watch, can’t find anything, and then decide to do something else. And 27.4 percent of respondents said that, on average, they either always or often try to watch a specific program but end up not watching it because they couldn’t find it on streaming.

“Through the lens of catalogue size and streaming usage, connecting the 83.4 percent who don’t always know what they want to watch should be fairly straightforward, especially as companies increasingly use customer data to create personalized experiences that foster long-term engagement and loyalty. Audiences, however, aren’t finding what they’re looking for, and content discovery challenges are mounting,” the report reads.

Forty-seven percent of participants said they spend more time looking for something to watch than they did a year ago. The average amount of time spent is 12 minutes.

In recent years, numerous streaming services have paid large sums to air live events, including sports, to grow their advertising businesses. However, “nearly” 52 percent of streaming users that Gracenote surveyed said they “frequently don’t know which channel or service a specific sports event is on.”

“Sports events that are behind subscription paywalls are often hidden from traditional search, which inhibits discovery,” the report notes.

And in an example of streaming users yearning for experiences most closely connected to traditional, broadcast television, Gracenote reported that almost 35 percent of respondents spend more time watching free ad-supported streaming television services, such as Tubi or The Roku Channel, because subscription-based alternatives, such as Netflix and Disney+, have “become overwhelming.”

The on-demand nature of streaming services has degraded the need for live/virtual and print-out TV listings, such as a TV Guide. But challenges in finding content on streaming services are apparently leading many streaming viewers to yearn for that classic form of guidance.

In Gracenote’s survey, 72.3 percent of respondents said they want “a consolidated guide to the programming available across all of the streaming services that they use.”

Streaming service providers and analysts I’ve spoken with have pointed to content discovery as a streaming challenge for years. Some, like Netflix and Amazon Prime Video, have turned to AI to help people search for content by using casual terms or to analyze viewing habits to make new recommendations. Others, such as Tubi, have turned to user-generated content playlists and recommendations.

However, there is apparently still a deficit when it comes to good and accessible streaming recommendations, as 31 percent of survey respondents said they have yet to find a “good source of recommendations for their personal taste and preferences.”

Despite there being hundreds of streaming services—as analysis this month from research firm Parks and Associates this month states—many would agree with Springsteen that it still feels like there’s “nothin’ to watch.”

#353. Nvidia chaos, Dem sweep, lawsuits, protests: AIs terrible, no-good November

Investors and industry experts have long worried that the AI industry will expand until it inevitably bursts. And in the wake of a historic midterm election cycle that decidedly challenged the interests of Big Tech, the AI industry is facing enormous strain from all sides.

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, a leading figure of the AI boom, has gone down in a salacious scandal involving extramarital affairs and accusations of Chinese espionage. SpaceXSI owner Elon Musk invested and lost millions on Republican campaigns during the midterms that he and other Big Tech leaders hoped would open up the floodgates to greater AI infrastructure. OpenAI and Anthropic have been implicated in numerous cybersecurity scandals on top of a domestic mental health crisis. Google, Meta, and OpenAI's ChatGPT are at the epicenter of a child safety reckoning that could change education and social media forever.

The latest challenge the industry is facing down is a lawsuit filed today (Nov. 12) by a safety and civil society groups Public Citizen, Irreplaceable Education Fund, Heat Initiative, and The Alliance for Secure AI. The groups argue that AI's developers are aware of "imminent and catastrophic risks to humanity" posed by AI and urges courts to freeze the industry's development completely in the name of public safety.

"Despite endless warnings about the grave risks their products pose to public safety and even civilization itself, AI company executives are rushing to develop and deploy ever-more advanced AI systems," wrote Robert Weissman, co-president of Public Citizen, in a press release. "We the People should not simply stand by and tolerate AI executives’ unhinged risk-taking at the public’s expense. Our lawsuit asks the court to apply existing law and find the AI corporations’ reckless practices unlawful and freeze further AI development."

The group argues that companies are risking public safety by developing and releasing products they have admitted are potentially harmful, and that further development can be halted under California's Unfair Competition Law. Originally published November 12, 2026

Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang has resigned following revelations that he had two extramarital affairs, plunging the chipmaker at the heart of the global AI boom into crisis.

The 63-year-old will be replaced by chief financial officer Colette Kress, who will serve as interim chief executive.

His exit comes after he admitted to having a relationship with a woman that US authorities believe was a Chinese spy. It also emerged on Wednesday that Huang had an affair with a senior Nvidia executive.

Nvidia shares were down more than 10 percent in pre-market trading, extending earlier losses after the announcement of Huang’s resignation.

Huang, who cofounded Nvidia in 1993, led it to become what was until recently the world’s most valuable company.

Huang’s departure marks a startling end to his more than 33-year tenure, during which Nvidia evolved from a small video-game chip company into the linchpin of the global AI infrastructure boom.

Huang has become one of the most recognizable figures in tech, as Nvidia’s semiconductors became integral to training and running AI systems such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT and Anthropic’s Claude.

Nvidia stock extended its pre-market decline after the company announced that Jensen Huang had resigned as chief executive.

Shares were down 10.2 percent in pre-market trading, a move that would wipe off more than $400 billion in market capitalization.

#347. Haaretz Expands US Presence Amid Israel’s Move to the Right

Haaretz has more than doubled the size of its American bureau, including hiring correspondents to cover beats like national politics and young American Jews.

Haaretz has more than doubled the size of its American bureau, including hiring correspondents to cover beats like national politics and young American Jews.

Allison Kaplan Sommer, the host of Haaretz’s English-language podcast, said she saw the paper connecting liberal-leaning and secular American Jews with their Israeli counterparts.Credit...Avishag Shaar-Yashuv for The New York Times

Marc Tracy reported from Tel Aviv and New York.

The century-old newspaper Haaretz has long been favored by Israel’s more liberal readers, its journalism and commentary often casting a harsh light on right-wing leaders and government policies toward Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank.

But its editorial outlook is increasingly out of step with a country that has moved rightward in recent decades — something seen as recently as in last month’s elections, in which a healthy majority of parliamentary seats went to center, right-of-center and Orthodox Jewish parties. At the same time, the digital edition of Haaretz’s English-language journalism has experienced growing popularity as an increasing number of Jews around the world, especially younger ones, view Israel more critically than in the past.

It has more than doubled the size of its small U.S. bureau, including hiring four correspondents to cover beats like national politics and a younger generation of American Jews, and is making more vertical videos and podcast episodes to reflect the country’s news consumption habits. In recent weeks, Haaretz’s reporters filed dispatches from Maine and Michigan, Texas and Florida, and Georgia and New York exploring the midterm races from Jewish perspectives.

And Haaretz is highlighting original English-language journalism that helps explain Israel to readers abroad. Its flagship English-language podcast, for instance, has grown its listenership by two-thirds this year with weekly election-themed episodes and overviews of Israel-based reporting, such as a recent interview with the authors of an investigation into a network of right-wing Israeli think tanks.

“We’re trying to think about ourselves not only as an Israeli newspaper, but also as a Jewish newspaper,” said Noa Landau, who was appointed this year to be global editor.

#346. United is restricting AI agents from booking flights

The airline believes AI poses security risks and may violate DOT and FAA rules.

Giving something like your payment details or log-in credentials to an agent can expose sensitive information and lead to "unintended purchases, account changes or use of MileagePlus miles," United says. Allowing an AI agent to complete a booking could also violate existing airline regulations from the Department of Transportation and Federal Aviation Administration. For example, United says only a human can acknowledge that their bag doesn't contain explosives during flight check-in.

United would like to avoid association with any agent-based embarrassments, for instance when Meta's Muse leaked a user's address online. Like other companies, it also wants to keep AI from getting in the way of its relationship with customers. The Wall Street Journal reports that United CEO Scott Kirby has been open about the company's reluctance to jump into agentic commerce. "To be honest, I don't want to create another middleman," Kirby said at an event on Tuesday. Not every airline feels the same way: Southwest created a plugin so users can book flights with ChatGPT.

Luckily for United and other companies blocking AI agents, agentic commerce might not be taking off with regular people. During its recent third-quarter earnings call, Uber shared that it handled 320 million trips and deliveries during the week of Halloween; of those, AI agents were responsible for 500 Uber Eats orders and only four rides. Those aren't exactly industry-destroying numbers.

Handing an AI your credit card is very different from handing it your email login, and both are different from just asking it to send you a news summary every morning. Even if a large number of people are downloading apps like Muse, it seems like letting an agent shop for you isn't as frictionless or foolproof as it needs to be to catch on.

#345. What is Microsoft Exchange? The cloud tool behind Microsoft Outlook issues.

Microsoft's popular suite of enterprise products, known as Microsoft 365, which includes Outlook, Teams, and Copilot, suffered from a massive outage on Thursday.

While more than 100,000 users reported problems accessing Microsoft Outlook and Microsoft 365 on the platform DownDetector, Microsoft is saying it's actually a cloud tool called Microsoft Exchange that's causing problems.

So, what is Microsoft Exchange? And why does it being down affect your ability to access your email or message your coworkers? The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Microsoft Exchange is the backend cloud tool that sends data updates to Outlook. It's essentially the server that stores the actual data, handles the mail flow, syncs the data to services like Outlook, and controls account security policies. Put simply, Outlook is the front end; Exchange is the back end.

For most users, though, this is a distinction without a difference. The end result is that users can't access their email, calendar, or messages.

#344. Google pushes privacy angle as Gemini-powered smartglasses debut starting at $395

The first Gemini smartglasses are a collab between Google, Samsung, Gentle Monster, and Warby Parker.

These devices are a collaboration between Google, Samsung, and the aforementioned eyewear designers. Gentle Monster and Warby Parker have each designed a few different frames, with options for transparent and tinted lenses. Samsung is providing the computing hardware that fits inside them, and Google has the software covered.

Warby Parker has designed three frames for Google eyewear, called Alden, Reid, and Wren (see above). They are available with both standard optical and sun lenses in Pine, Saddle, and Jet Black colors at $395 and higher. Warby Parker is only selling devices in the US. Gentle Monster has gone a bit further with six styles, but Google hasn’t told us any names for those yet. They come in dark red, pink, gray, and yellow colors and are available with clear, tinted, blue, or brown lenses. The Gentle Monster styles start a little higher at $449, and they will be available in the US and South Korea.

Google says the frames all weigh about 41 grams, which is not terrible but is definitely near the top for glasses generally. They will, naturally, weigh much more when you account for lenses. That will also determine the final pricing. So far, we only have the starting prices for these devices, including non-prescription lenses.

The setup here is similar to the Ray-Ban Meta glasses we’ve seen for a few years. There are no screens in the lenses, but you can interact with the AI system via voice or by activating the integrated camera. The experience should be similar to using Gemini Live on your phone, but it will be a lot easier to fire up the AI. You can say “Hey Gemini” or tap a touch-sensitive area of the frame to start interacting with the robot.

Credit: Gentle Monster Credit: Gentle Monster

Since this is the same chatbot that operates across the Google ecosystem, it has access to your data and preferences. You can ask questions and get personalized answers, have Google Maps guide you by voice, or just talk with the AI to kill time. The glasses use custom open-ear speakers to limit distractions for those around you. Google notes the directional microphones should not pick up speech from other people, either.

Adding the camera feed is where things get interesting. When the AI can see what you see, it has the potential to do more work on your behalf. Google suggests asking Gemini for recommendations when looking at a bookshelf or glancing at an event poster to have the AI add it to your calendar. The glasses will also support Gemini Guided Vision, which provides conversational audio descriptions for people who are visually impaired.

The utility of these devices hinges on Gemini. I would be remiss not to point out that Gemini, like all AI systems, is not infallible. In fact, it can be frustratingly fallible at times. The chatbot still regularly screws up simple things like adding calendar appointments or identifying objects in an image in my testing, and when you don’t have a screen to verify the AI’s output, it may be harder to trust it. These devices will really only make sense if you can rely on Gemini to do things right, and that’s still not a guarantee.

For starters, there’s a recording light that is on whenever the camera is active. Like the Meta glasses, Google’s eyewear will detect if the light has been covered up or modified in any way that would prevent it from illuminating. In that event, the glasses will refuse to activate the camera. All Google-powered glasses will have a physical off switch, too.

Credit: Warby Parker Credit: Warby Parker

The terms of use for Gemini eyewear also require users to agree not to record people without consent or in sensitive areas. If Google finds out someone has broken those rules, it may remotely disable the glasses. Google is going a bit further in restricting video functionality than Meta, too. Google says it will not allow any apps in the Play Store to live stream video to social media from the glasses (not even YouTube live is supported). The content you record is saved to your gallery, and you can upload that wherever you want, but going live is apparently not on the agenda. Real-time facial recognition is also off the table, according to Google.  The glasses also automatically redact faces before sending video to Google for processing.

Data on the glasses is encrypted, and it remains that way during transit to the cloud. However, it’s not encrypted on Google’s servers. The company does note that it’s working to bring Private AI Compute to glasses, which will isolate data during processing so not even Google can access it.

Credit: Gentle Monster Credit: Gentle Monster

Turkey’s global ambitions are written all over the walls of the sprawling conference center in Antalya, the Mediterranean resort city where it is host to the United Nations climate summit that opened on Monday.

“Turkey, where dialogue becomes action,” read giant signs throughout the venue.

“The Future of the World is in Turkey!” read others.

President Recep Tayyip Erdogan has long sought to position Turkey as a rising economic and military power that can mediate conflicts and play a muscular role in international affairs. Welcoming the tens of thousands of officials, ecologists, activists and others attending the U.N. summit, known as COP31, gives the country an opportunity to extend its diplomatic efforts into the critical realm of climate diplomacy, experts said.

They say the country lacks a clear timeline for phasing out coal, continues to import large amounts of plastic waste from Europe and is heavy-handed in its treatment of residents who oppose mining projects.

The climate summit comes months after Turkey hosted its fellow members of the NATO military alliance in the Turkish capital, Ankara, where it showcased its value as a defense partner in an unstable region and the bonhomie between Mr. Erdogan and President Trump.

Now, Turkey seeks to cast itself as a key player in international climate talks, too.

“Turkey wants to show itself as a global power in many different places, including for peace, for climate, for trade,” said Umit Sahin, who heads the climate change program at Istanbul Policy Center, a research institute.

#339. The amazing disappearing exoplanet

An exoplanet seems to have stopped transiting in front of its host star.

One nice thing about discovering exoplanets is that, once you work out how long their orbits take, they continue to show up like clockwork. The timing is so regular, in fact, that we can use variations to detect the gravitational influence of additional planets in the same exosolar system. And the precise timing is useful in that we can rely on it to point telescopes at the system when we know some of the light we detect has passed through the planet’s atmosphere.

On Monday, researchers reported on an exoplanet that simply stopped showing up. TOI-2535b is a hot Jupiter-sized planet we’ve been observing as it transits between its host star, TOI-2535, and Earth for about seven years now. But it recently stopped showing up, and researchers suspect that’s due to an unseen companion orbiting in a different plane.

TOI-2535 is one of the many stars with exoplanet candidates identified by TESS, the Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite. It’s a K dwarf, meaning it’s somewhat smaller than the Sun. And TESS found signs of a Jupiter-sized planet orbiting it every eight days, an orbit close enough to make TOI-2535b a “hot Jupiter” exoplanet. At that point, there was nothing especially unusual about it.

But a closer look at the data showed two very strange things. First, the timing of when its transits occurred could shift by as much as six hours, a substantial variation for an orbit this brief. That’s typically a sign of gravitational interactions with another planet; depending on the details of that second planet’s orbit, it may slow the first one or accelerate it through a transit more quickly. Since the second planet doesn’t show up in TESS data, it appears to orbit in a slightly different plane, so it doesn’t pass between its host star and Earth.

The second oddity is that the transit duration grew progressively shorter in more recent observations. This suggests that, while early observations saw the planet transiting across the star’s equator, later ones involved transits near one of the poles, ensuring that the planet spent less time between the star and Earth. This trend eventually reached its logical end point: TOI-2535b simply stopped transiting in the most recent observations.

Obviously, gravitational interactions with a planet orbiting in a different plane could potentially alter TOI-2535b’s orbit so it no longer passes between TOI-2535 and Earth. There is also some precedent for the converse happening: a star around which no transits were originally detected but where transits began appearing in later observations. Still, as a Jupiter-sized planet, TOI-2535b should not be that easy to shift, and as the researchers note, “This is the fastest rate of inclination change observed for an exoplanet orbiting a single star.”

The second, unseen planet “would need to be either extremely massive or on a highly eccentric orbit,” the researchers noted. So they decided to figure out what sorts of configurations could lead to such rapid changes.

To do so, they modeled 300,000 different starting configurations and ran them forward in time. They found there wasn’t a single favored configuration; as long as the planets were in an orbital resonance, things were relatively stable, at least over relatively short periods. So they took the 100 best solutions and ran them forward for a million years.

About two-thirds became unstable during that period, which ruled out a couple of the potential resonances. The remaining third shared a couple of features. One is that the second, undetected planet has to be pretty massive—roughly Saturn-sized at a minimum. The second is that the angle between the orbits of the two planets can’t be that large. If it is, the difference would shrink over time, causing their orbits to become increasingly eccentric. Eventually, this results in a close approach between the two planets, followed by instability.

The University of California, Los Angeles has posted a video of one of the better models if you’re having a hard time visualizing all of this.

While a second planet hasn’t been detected via the transit method, its gravitational pull on the star should create changes in the radial velocity we could potentially detect. Right now, however, we don’t have enough clean data to detect it. The researchers also say there’s enough noise in the transit timing variations that a third planet in the system can’t be excluded.

The question we probably can’t answer with further observations is how this system got into its current state. Large planets are thought to form farther out from the star and then migrate inward because of friction caused by the gas in the planet-forming disk. (This inward motion tends to stop when orbital resonances develop.) But this friction should also limit the angle between the planets’ orbits. Without some additional event that mixed up the orbits, it’s not obvious how this system could have come to be.

#338. New York is forcing T-Mobile to stop putting data caps on low-income home internet plans

The company was throttling speeds for plans offered under the Affordable Broadband Act.

New York's attorney general found that T-Mobile was not following the state's Affordable Broadband Act, which sets requirements for offering service to low-income residents. Under the ABA, eligible households must be able to purchase broadband at speeds of at least 25 Mbps for $15 a month or 200 Mbps for $20 a month without additional taxes or equipment fees. Providers are also required to make "reasonable efforts" to promote these low-cost options.

The Office of the Attorney General discovered that T-Mobile had placed monthly data caps on its ABA plans. According to the press release, T-Mobile would throttle speeds to as low as 650 kilobits per second after a customer had used 50GB of data, which could happen in about four days of typical broadband use. The company was also only offering its ABA plans in areas where it had 5G wireless deployed and was preventing information about those plans on its website from being seen by search engines.

Following the investigation, T-Mobile will remove those data caps, make its ABA plans available in all places in the state where it offers home internet service, and will ensure that search engines can access details about ABA plans.

"Reliable and affordable internet service is a basic need for all New Yorkers, but T-Mobile flouted the law designed to help vulnerable New Yorkers access it," Attorney General Letitia James said. "My office is committed to seeing the Affordable Broadband Act realize its goal of ensuring that New Yorkers in need are not denied access to affordable home internet. I encourage all eligible New Yorkers to enroll in a low-cost home internet plan through the ABA."

In a statement shared with Engadget, T-Mobile the company said "We have a long history of helping customers in New York stay connected through low-cost broadband, and we're updating our plan to meet the state's requirements while maintaining the same low price. We've reached an agreement with the New York Attorney General that builds on that commitment, provides greater clarity around our offering and makes the plan available to more eligible customers."

#336. In Argentina, Pope Leo Walks in the Giant Shadow of Francis

Francis never returned to his home country after becoming pope, but the new pontiff will still be confronted with the overwhelming presence of his predecessor.

A swimming pool named after Pope Francis in La Matanza, outside Buenos Aires.Credit...

When the Argentine priest was reminded that the Roman Catholic Church no longer had an Argentine pope, he let out an exasperated chuckle.

“We are already mourning Messi,” said the priest, the Rev. Lorenzo de Vedia, referring to Lionel Messi’s departure from the Argentine national soccer team. “Two is too much.”

Though Pope Francis, the first Argentine pope, died over a year ago and was succeeded by Pope Leo shortly after, Argentines have hardly moved on. Francis’ photo still adorns public schools, parishes and trade unions. Murals and mosaics of the Argentine pope dominate public spaces, street vendors still sell his figurines, parish priests quote him in their homilies.

With Leo visiting the country, Argentines are finally confronting the reality that the leader of the Catholic Church no longer speaks Porteño, the Buenos Aires dialect; roots for the city’s beloved San Lorenzo team; or shares their ritual of mate — the national drink.

As Argentines face this new reality, their reception is permeated with the pressing question of whether Leo represents an expression of Francis’ continuity. And while the answer is important to the former pope’s compatriots, it is a question much of the world has been asking.

#335. Nintendo is ending Google and Apple sign-ins early next year

The company is asking users to set up a password or a passkey for their Nintendo accounts.

Nintendo will discontinue Google and Apple log-ins early next year, so you may want to update the way you sign into your account soon. Starting on January 26, 2027, it will no longer be possible to sign into your Nintendo account, or to create a new one, with Google and Apple as a log-in method. In addition, it is also ending the My Nintendo Platinum Points mission that rewards you for linking your Google with your Nintendo account.

The company had made the announcement in a support page, where it also said that the exact end time of Google and Apple sign-ins depend "on the progress of the system change work." Nintendo is asking users to set up a password or a passkey before January 26. To set up a passkey, go to the Accounts page, sign into your account, choose Sign-In and Security Settings, select Passkeys and then Edit. From there, you can choose Register New Passkey to start signing into Nintendo with your device's authentication method.

Nintendo didn't say why it's removing Google and Apple sign-ins, and whether it has security concerns with them. Whatever Nintendo's reason for it, you may want to get your log-in sorted if you want uninterrupted access to your games and to the eShop.

#334. Who could replace Jensen Huang at Nvidia long-term? Here are the top contenders.

After reports of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, Jensen Huang is out at Nvidia.

With Huang's resignation from the chipmaking giant that he helped found, who will take the helm at Nvidia?

While Nvidia quickly announced an interim CEO in Colette Kress, there's a lot of speculation about who will take a more permanent role as head of one of the world's most valuable companies.

First up is Nvidia's longtime Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, already a billionaire in her own right.

Kress technically has the CEO job right now, as Nvidia paired Huang's resignation announcement with the news that Kress would fill in as interim CEO. Before being promoted to the interim CEO position, Kress has worked as the CFO at the company since 2013. She previously worked as CFO for divisions at Microsoft and Cisco. Nvidia's investors are familiar with her too from the company's quarterly earnings calls, so she may have the upper hand there too.

Kress may have the advantage of being the interim CEO, but Ian Buck seems to be the favorite amongst the speculators.

Like Kress, Buck would also be an internal choice for CEO. Buck has worked at Nvidia since 2004 and is currently the company's Vice President and General Manager of Hyperscale and High-Performance Computing (HPC).

In his role at Nvidia, Buck is responsible for the company's data center, cloud, and HPC product lines — all central to Nvidia's success amid the AI boom.

In addition, Buck is credited as the creator of CUDA, or Compute Unified Device Architecture. CUDA is Nvidia's proprietary platform that developers use to run their software on Nvidia's GPUs.

#333. Man's secret use of synthetic testosterone for cows nearly destroyed his heart

Doctors warn that such life-threatening cases are becoming more common.

For 11 years, a Massachusetts man had an alarming array of health problems, most notably that his heart kept failing. He landed in the hospital over and over again, needed life support at one point, spent years going to specialists, and underwent a barrage of tests along the way.

But through it all, he never revealed a key detail behind his ailments: He was taking powerful, illicit synthetic testosterone derivatives (aka anabolic-androgenic steroids, or AAS). One of the drugs he took was approved only for use in animals; it’s primarily used to beef up cows.

Left in the dark, his doctors had to puzzle this out on their own. In a case report in this week’s issue of the New England Journal of Medicine, they explain how they did it. They also offer a warning: These cases will only become more common, and clinicians need to know what they look like.

“Use of AAS has now become a major international substance use disorder,” they wrote. In the US, it’s estimated that between 2.9 and 4 million people, mostly male athletes, use AAS. To put this in a public health perspective, this is more than the number of people in the US with an HIV infection.

“Clinicians should be prepared to encounter increasing numbers of patients presenting with the long-term effects of AAS exposure, including cardiomyopathy, atherosclerotic disease, and anabolic steroid–induced hypogonadism.”

Patients also need to be prepared for what recovery looks like, which they described as being “lengthy and varied.” The man’s case offers a stark example.

The problems started when he was 39. He fell ill after returning from a trip to the Caribbean and went to Massachusetts General Hospital in Boston with a fever, chills, and trouble breathing. Tests suggested he had an acute kidney injury, liver damage, and an injured heart. Testing of his heart showed that the left chamber was pumping out just 21 percent of its blood in a beat (a normal amount is above 50 percent). His heart only got worse from there, and within hours, he ended up on a type of life support called ECMO (venoarterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation), a machine that does the work of both the heart and lungs in dire circumstances. He luckily recovered—and quickly. But doctors were baffled by the cause.

When he was weaned off ECMO a few days later, his heart function normalized. Doctors looked for evidence of anything that could have sparked the sudden cardiac spiral. They found nothing but a mildly elevated level of antibodies against a toxin made by Streptococcus bacteria. In rare instances, an infection with these germs can lead to a condition called poststreptococcal myocarditis, which could potentially explain some of his symptoms. It was their best guess. They prescribed him antibiotics, as well as drugs to lower his blood pressure.

Two years later, he went to another hospital. This time it was for acute pain in his abdomen and legs. Doctors found signs of cell death in his kidneys. The arteries that supply blood to his kidneys appeared narrowed, suggesting a condition called renal-artery stenosis. There were also signs of inflammation in his blood vessels. But the doctors at the hospital couldn’t pinpoint the problem. They prescribed him anti-inflammatory drugs and sent him on his way.

For the next five years, the man started seeing a cadre of specialists to monitor his health, including doctors in primary care, cardiology, nephrology, rheumatology, general medicine, sleep medicine, and psychiatry. When he was about 46, he reported fatigue to his primary care doctor, who found that his testosterone level was low. The man then went to an endocrinologist, reporting not just fatigue but low libido and poor sleep. The endocrinologist recommended treatment for obstructive sleep apnea, which can increase the risk of low testosterone.

A year later, the man added a hematologist to his rotation of care providers. A routine blood test revealed abnormally high levels of red blood cells (erythrocytosis). The hematologist noted the man also had high blood pressure (150/108 mm Hg) but found everything else to be normal. The hematologist ended up attributing the erythrocytosis to the man’s sleep apnea and renal-artery stenosis.

It wasn’t until he arrived again at Mass General in 2026 that doctors finally put it all together. Now 50, the man was once again experiencing heart failure—and he had again just returned from the Caribbean.

In the case report, doctors provided a more complete picture of the man, noting that he lived with his wife and children in eastern Massachusetts, worked as a facilities manager, and described himself as a workout “fanatic.” He used tanning beds and applied spray tans. He told doctors he had erectile dysfunction and reduced libido for several years. They also noticed that he had mildly enlarged breast tissue (a condition called gynecomastia), progressive insulin resistance, decreased levels of “good” cholesterol (HDL or high-density lipoprotein), and fatty liver disease.

When he arrived at the hospital, he reported five days of worsening chest tightness and pressure, fatigue, swelling in his legs, and trouble breathing. His heart was racing at 134 beats per minute. His blood pressure was high at 146/106 mm Hg. Doctors described him as “muscular, tanned, anxious, and in mild distress.” Tests showed his heart was once again failing, with the left chamber pumping out just 20 percent of its blood.

The doctors started reviewing a long list of possible explanations. They ruled out infectious diseases that can cause heart inflammation (myocarditis), as well as autoimmune conditions, medication side effects, and toxins. The explanation they were left with—which also seemed to explain much of his other problems—was high doses of testosterone, or perhaps an AAS.

Illicit use of AAS is clearly linked to the heart problems, including the decreased blood pumping the man had, as well as strain and damage. It could also explain his enlarged breast tissue, fatty liver and signs of liver damage, progressive insulin resistance, kidney injuries, high blood pressure, and high levels of red blood cells (erythrocytosis). The sleep problems, fatigue, and low testosterone levels—which doctors previously guessed were from obstructive sleep apnea—could actually be from a drop in natural hormone production after doses of AAS (a condition called hypogonadism).

In the man’s lengthy medical history, he had never mentioned using AAS or testosterone supplements. Doctors prepared to have a delicate conversation to determine if he was secretly using the drugs. When they finally broached the topic, he confessed; he had been intermittently using high doses of AAS to boost his muscles, taking the most recent dose before his vacation to the Caribbean. The testosterone derivative he used was trenbolone, an AAS only approved for use in veterinary medicine; it’s mostly used in livestock.

The doctors then tested his total testosterone level, finding it was so high that the test couldn’t accurately measure it. The assay’s range of detection was 12 to 1,500 nanograms per deciliter of blood. His level was over 1,500 ng/dl. The range considered normal for the test was between 249 and 836 ng/dl. The doctors gave their final diagnosis: androgen excess–related cardiomyopathy.

The primary treatment for his heart is to stop taking AAS. Doctors prescribed him medications for high blood pressure, heart failure, and diabetes. Four months later, heart tests showed his left chamber was pumping 49 percent of its blood, a significant improvement. The state of his liver also improved. But recovery from the AAS-induced hypogonadism was a much longer, more difficult journey. It was made harder because he was over age 35, had taken AAS for more than a decade, and had preexisting dysfunction.

Six months into his recovery, he still had significant hypogonadism symptoms, which include loss of sexual desire, erectile dysfunction, gynecomastia, reduced orgasmic satisfaction, and ejaculatory disorders. He opted to try a drug called clomiphene, which binds to estrogen receptors. It’s often used in women to help with fertility, but in men it is sometimes used to try to jumpstart natural testosterone production. It didn’t work for the man. He moved on to trying topical testosterone-replacement therapy but continued to have erectile dysfunction. Doctors warn that it could take up to three years for the hypogonadism to resolve.

In the last follow-up at eight months, he reported that he was still off AAS and had no symptoms of heart failure.
