#331. Apple TV 4K (4th gen) review: Still great, but drastically more expensive

The $150 price increase is hard to swallow.

The Apple TV 4K has been our pick for the best premium streaming device since the third-gen model arrived in 2022. In terms of pure performance, it was undoubtedly the best you could buy, thanks to the combination of picture quality, speed and features you wouldn't find on any of the more affordable competition. That was true four years ago and it's still true now.

Unfortunately, the global tech landscape has changed massively over the last two years. Nearly every device is more expensive, especially if it needs RAM or its own processor. Apple's streaming box didn't escape that fate. For the 4th-generation Apple TV 4K, Apple made big upgrades to performance and added a slew of new features, and of course, it carved out a more prominent place for Siri AI in your living room. The compact streaming box is a lot more expensive than the previous generation cost at launch, though, and the new $299 price tag certainly dulls its shine.

If you were hoping for a new design, there are only subtle tweaks to report. The most evident is that Apple moved the light on the front of the box from the right to the center. If you dive into the spec sheets, you'll discover the new model is slightly larger than the previous version. I'm talking 0.24mm bigger in width and depth, and 3 grams heavier. You'll be forgiven for not noticing. The Siri Remote, however, is exactly the same size at 1.4 inches x 5.4 inches x 0.36 inches.

Apple upgraded the processor from the A15 Bionic to the A19 chip. In iPhone terms, that would be like going from an iPhone 13 to an iPhone 17, so the computing power increase here is significant. The company says that the A19 chip is capable of 50 percent faster CPU performance and up to 2x faster GPU performance than the previous Apple TV 4K. I don't have the tools to quantifiably check those claims, but I can tell you the device is super responsive, navigation is speedy and overall the UI is noticeably more fluid. Plus, the A19 delivers a dedicated AV1 decoder for HDR rendering efficiency.

Of course, the A19 offers a boost to Siri AI, which is playing a bigger role in the living room thanks to the new Apple TV 4K. For starters, the streaming box now has three far-field microphones built in, so you no longer have to use the remote to interact with the assistant. You can simply speak your queries while watching TV and Siri is off and running. Like before, Apple's AI can help with finding and playing live TV, movies, shows and sports from your go-to apps. But with Siri's recently expanded capabilities, it can now pull in personal context from Messages and other apps too. So when you want Siri to "play the show Jack texted me about," the assistant knows just what to do. Additionally, audio captured from the far-field mics is processed within the Secure Exclave on the A19, so neither the apps, the OS nor Apple get access to any of the data.

The new feature that will get the most use at my house is Precision Finding for the remote in the Find My app. I have two kids who are constantly leaving the remote in undisclosed locations, so the added ability to play a sound has already greatly reduced the amount of time I spend searching for it. And when I can't hear it, Precision Finding is better than the previous method for locating the remote with an iPhone. With the built-in mics for Siri though, a lost remote is much less frustrating since you can just tell the Apple TV 4K what to do.

There's also a noteworthy audio upgrade on the Apple TV 4K. Before, you could only use two HomePod or HomePod mini speakers as a stereo pair. Now you can use up to four of those, so long as they're second-gen HomePods or HomePod mini 2s, for a more complete home theater surround sound system. The only additional requirement is that the two front speakers and two rear speakers have to match. So you can have two HomePods in the front and two HomePod mini 2s in the back, which is the configuration I tested. If you have four of the same speaker, that works too.

Once you have all of the HomePods in place, you'll hear a series of tones as the Apple TV 4K locates the speakers and tunes the audio for the room. I suspected that a combination of HomePods and HomePod minis is the ideal setup and my testing proved me right. The HomePods provide the added bass needed for action scenes, or really just for a fuller soundstage most TV viewing will eventually require. The HomePod minis can't do that on their own, but they do well as rear speakers, providing dimensional backup to the larger speakers at the front. I think having four HomePods would be overkill, not to mention expensive. I also heard a four-mini configuration during a demo last month, and while it's certainly a boost compared to TV speakers, it's not nearly as potent as the arrangement I tested at home.

I'll also note that there's a feature called Whole Room Audio that will be great for parties and gatherings. This allows for all four speakers to play the same sounds, rather than the fragments each might deliver in a surround sound setup. This ensures that everyone hears everything, from vocals to guitars, or even the commentators of an F1 race. Whole Room Audio is also easy to activate as it's accessible from the Speaker Mode option in the Control Center.

Like it has in the past, the new Apple TV 4K can function as a smart home hub. This allows you to control HomeKit and Matter accessories like cameras, locks and thermostats, and Apple says "thousands" of such devices are compatible. Thanks to the HomeKit Secure Video, you can expect secure local processing on the streaming box, which encrypts clips before they're stored in iCloud. Apple Intelligence provides captions for camera feeds and you can control everything from the Control Center.

Siri voice control is available as well, as is the ability to run scenes from the Home app in the Control Center. If you have a HomeKit or Matter compatible camera (I don't), you can get a live view of those in the top right corner of your TV screen. If someone rings the doorbell, you can also choose to be automatically notified on the display you're already looking at. If needed, you can chat with them and unlock the door without leaving your seat.

My biggest complaint about the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K is the price. At $299, this model is $150 more than the equivalent version of its predecessor was at launch. Sure, Apple increased the price of the third-gen Apple TV 4K from $149 to $249 earlier this year, and a lot has happened since that model debuted in 2022. However, even with rising memory and component costs, tacking on another $50 to the already inflated price tag is adding insult to injury. It especially stings when you consider the company actually decreased prices when it launched these models in 2022.

To pour more salt in the wound, Apple got rid of the cheaper Wi-Fi-only option that also offered less storage space (64GB). In 2022, that version of the third-gen Apple TV 4K launched for $129, though it eventually went up to $199 this year. If you'll allow me to dig even deeper into Apple TV 4K history, that $129 model was not only $50 cheaper than the W-Fi-only version before it, but even doubled the 32GB onboard storage. Its original price was a drop from the $179 that the comparative version preceding it cost. Meanwhile, the fourth-gen Apple TV 4K only comes in one configuration and it has 128GB of storage; that's the same capacity as the priciest model of the previous generation.

After living with the Apple TV 4K's new home theater setup with four HomePods, I'm starting to question how many people will actually use it. Not only does it require you to have four of Apple's speakers, but you'll also have to put all four of them in the same space. When you consider one of the primary functions of a HomePod is to serve as a direct line to Siri, permanently putting all four in your living room seems like a waste. Not to mention you'll miss out on multiroom audio and features like Intercom, which are more helpful if your HomePods are spread around the house. After some testing, it seems to me like the quad-Pod speaker arrangement is a neat bonus — something you temporarily pull together for movie night or parties — rather than a substitute for a soundbar or traditional home theater setup.

I have another gripe that is more of a frustration with tvOS as a whole, but it's worth mentioning as it relates to the new Apple TV 4K. Even if you've added the streaming box to the Home app and assigned it to a room, the Home icon won't appear in the Control Center until you've either added a camera or created a Scene. So if you fire up your Apple TV and can't find your non-camera devices, that's probably why.

I appreciate the fact that camera feeds will be the most useful on a TV. But I think it's silly that I have to create a Scene (manually triggered saved settings) in order to see things like a thermostat or lights on a device that's advertised as a "smart home hub." At the very least, I want there to be a shortcut in the Control Center for Rooms I've set up in the Home app on iOS, where I can then access individual devices if needed. I should also mention that tvOS doesn't have a dedicated Home app; everything to do with controlling your smart home requires digging into the Control Center on an Apple TV. Or, of course, you could fire up the full app on your phone.

In terms of performance, the Apple TV 4K remains the best streaming box you can buy. That's been true for a while now, and the upgrades the company made for this version only cements its position at the top of the premium heap. However, with the current state of global technology, specifically the price of anything that uses RAM or a chip like the A19, Apple's streaming device isn't as easy to recommend as it once was.

If you're rocking with a third-gen model, I'd urge you to stay put. And if you're looking to invest in an Apple TV for the first time, try to hold out for a sale or catch one on the refurbished market. Heck, I'd even keep my eye out for a used third-gen model. Yes, you'll miss out on the newest features in that scenario, including the convenience of an always-listening Siri device that isn't a HomePod, but the core of what makes an Apple TV a great device is still there. And in late 2026, there's something to be said for saving significant money on a tech purchase.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:22 p.m. ET — The outage continued into the evening, as Microsoft provided its latest update: "We’re opening all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief."

Microsoft customers are reporting problems accessing a variety of the company's software products and services on Thursday afternoon.

#328. Building a rocket is hard. AST SpaceMobile thinks it's "simple."

“We’re not doing this thing because we like rockets.”

During an hour-long event on Thursday, Abel Avellan, the founder and chief executive of the telecommunications company AST SpaceMobile, laid out his vision for developing a large rocket to launch his Bluebird satellites into orbit.

Avellan emphasized, over and over again, that AST plans to build a “simple” rocket. He paraphrased SpaceX founder Elon Musk several times, saying, “the best part is no part.” Avellan emphasized that AST has already procured engines for its proposed Blacklift rocket, asserting that engine development accounts for 80 percent of a typical rocket’s development cycle.

“Of course rocket engineers are going to get mad with me, but you can simplify it as a big tank feeding engines,” he said of the rocket. “So big tanks—we know how to do them. We know how to do them. We know how to do them in composite.”

I am not a rocket engineer, and I am not mad at Avellan. But he sure seems to be substantially underestimating the challenge of building rockets.

Avellan also has a problem. AST SpaceMobile appears to be successful in building very large satellites, Bluebirds, that are capable of providing direct-to-cell capability from an orbit about 550km above Earth. The problem is there is a commercial launch crunch, and the company is struggling to get its satellites on orbit today, and projects to do so into the future.

“We’re not doing this thing because we like rockets,” he said during the presentation. “We’re not doing this thing because we want to be in the launch business. We want this because we have so much demand of our satellites, and as you have seen it, we cannot get enough launches.”

The Blacklift rocket will be “simple,” he said, because it will be solely tailored to lift five Bluebird satellites into low-Earth orbit at a time. It will have 20 metric tons of lift capacity, a reusable first stage, and launch twice a month from a pad at Wallops Flight Facility in Virginia that formerly served the Antares rocket. It will have all composite tanks and three stages. And, Avellan said, it will be ready to launch in 2028.

If that timeline sounds preposterous, that’s because it is.

However, Avellan believes AST has a workaround: It is committed to spending $1.3 billion to acquire enough engines for the first three Blacklift launches from Firefly Aerospace, as well as a license to manufacture additional engines over time. He is basing this argument on the Miranda first stage engine, which he said is “ready to fly.”

But that’s not quite true. However, Miranda has not yet completed its formal qualification campaign, much less flown on an orbital rocket. Firefly chief executive Jason Kim said this week that qualification testing remains underway. Moreover, little is known publicly about the readiness of the upper stage engine, Vira, that AST is also acquiring from Firefly for its rocket.

Another questionable assertion is Avellan’s statement that 80 percent of a rocket’s design cycle is due to engine development. “I mean, that has been proven,” he said.

I’m not sure it has. Propulsion is definitely one of the most challenging aspects of developing a rocket.

However, Rocket Lab, which has considerably more experience building orbital rockets than AST, has suffered two significant setbacks this year involving Neutron’s composite propellant tanks. These structures, as well as flight software, avionics, stage separation, payload fairings, qualification testing, and so much more, can lead to significant delays in the development of a rocket. And then there are the equally difficult challenges of scaling from development to production at rate, and recovering the first stage.

Avellan and Scott Wisniewski, president of AST SpaceMobile, also did not get into specifics about financing the development of Blacklift. The company has already invested more than $1 billion in engines and will likely need billions more to develop and scale the vehicle.

Wisniewski said AST SpaceMobile plans to spend “several hundred million dollars” in 2027 on Blacklift development, but it is not clear whether that includes milestone payments to Firefly for propulsion. According to the company’s balance sheet, it had about $2.8 billion in unrestricted cash and cash equivalents at the end of September, and it has historically been successful in raising additional capital.

The company’s underlying business plan makes sense. There is a potentially very large market for space-to-ground connectivity for mobile devices. Traditional mobile service operators are growing wary about working with SpaceX, viewing the company as a competitor rather than a partner. And the recent issues with Starlink in Ukraine may lead some nations to seek alternative partners for these services.

“First of all, I’m no Musk, and I don’t want to be,” Avellan said on Thursday. “We partner. We don’t compete. We enhance. … Why? Because we partner with them. We don’t compete with them. We don’t insult them. You know, we partner with them.”

SpaceX went from being a rocket company to operating satellites. Now Avellan and AST seek to go from being a satellite operator to launching rockets. Consider that SpaceX took eight years from its founding to launch the Falcon 9, another five years to successfully land a booster, and several more years to achieve a high flight rate. AST is seeking to compress much of that process into approximately two years.

But rocket science is in no way simple, and the road ahead is sure to be rocky. Avellan said AST doesn’t really want to be in the launch business. Given how difficult, demanding, and expensive the launch business is, he may get his wish in a few years.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#327. Apple HomeView review: A versatile smart display and Siri-powered home hub

The company’s smart screen debut heavily relies on an iPhone or iPad — for better and for worse.

Apple's entry into the smart display category was rumored for years. By the time its debut home hub device arrives this week, the company will be nearly a decade behind its main rivals in the space. In true Apple fashion, it refused to rush new products just because most of the competition was doing it. Now that the HomeView ($299) is here, Apple is hoping the iPhone faithful will like its mix of elevated design and newly expanded Siri capabilities.

Unlike smart displays from Amazon and Google, Apple's approach relies on a companion device. That means you'll need either an iPhone or an iPad to mirror apps using technology similar to CarPlay. As you'd imagine, that means the HomeView's usefulness, like CarPlay's, is directly tied to how much you use Apple's core apps — things like Messages, Calendar, Photos, Reminders and others. You'll also need to have some smart home devices to make the most of the smart display and be willing to use Safari as your recipe box. If all of that is OK to you, and if you're willing to give Siri access to personal data inside some of those apps, the HomeView can quickly become a very versatile device with something for your whole family.

I have fond memories of the iMac G4. It was the first computer I used in a design lab in college, and I loved how I could reposition the display to suit the task at hand. The first time I saw the HomeView, those memories flooded back. Apple's smart home display doesn't exactly replicate the G4 styling, but it's close enough for a decent nostalgia hit for me.

The HomeView essentially consists of two parts: a cylindrical, domed base and a 7-inch Liquid Retina display. At least, that's if you get the tabletop version. The wall-mounted option is just the screen, and you'll have to pay at least $59.95 for a mount. Every version of the HomeView has two full-range drivers in the display itself. The base houses additional components, like a down-firing woofer and a pair of force-canceling passive radiators for fuller sound with more bass than the wall-mounted models.

That display has an LED-backlit multi-touch IPS panel that offers a 1,088 x 1,088 resolution at 200 pixels per inch. Apple says it also added an oleophobic fingerprint-resistant coating to cut down on grime from everyday use. There's an ambient light sensor that enables the HomeView to adjust both brightness and warmth as needed. If it's in direct sunlight, you can expect the maximum 500 nits, but at night, it can dial things down to just 0.3 nits — the lowest brightness ever on an Apple display. The company believes this makes it perfect for a nightstand, if you're willing to have a camera pointed at or near your bed.

The HomeView's display has bezels like an iPad and the shape of a large Apple Watch. Unlike either of those devices, though, it has no physical buttons for controls. All of the interaction is done via swipes and taps on the touchscreen itself, or, as I'll elaborate on in a bit, with your voice. And unlike that iMac I'm nostalgic for, you can only tilt the display — you can't adjust its height. While that will suit most of your needs, I'm sure there are a lot of people who would've liked the ability to change the height or rotate the screen as well. Once you do adjust the way it tilts, a built-in accelerometer detects the new angle and alters the audio performance of the display's two drivers to match it.

Another item to discuss in terms of design is the HomeView's lone port. Around back, there's a single USB-C jack for power. Apple ships the standard version of the device with a USB-C to USB-C cable and a 30-watt power adapter. If you opt for the USB-C wall mount, which already costs $59.99, you'll need to purchase the power adapter separately. The company offers a 40W dynamic power adapter (up to 60W max) for $39 while the minimum required power supply (20W) will cost you $19. Obviously, the AC wired and PoE (power over Ethernet) wired mounts won't need a power adapter.

And lastly, there's the matter of color. For the standard (table or countertop) version of HomeView, you can choose from Silver, Starlight, Rose Pink or Space Gray options. On those, the sides and back of the display match the base. For the wall-mounted version, you can only pick between Silver and Space Gray. And, just in case you need the reminder, both HomeView models are the same price ($299).

One of the HomeView's most helpful traits is its ability to put Siri AI front and center in your home. The smart device runs on Apple's A18 chip, the processor that debuted with the iPhone 16 line in 2024. The company is using it here for its Secure Exclaves which give it the ability to process camera and microphone feeds on-device in a manner that can't be accessed by Apple. The company explained that this enables the HomeView to recognize your face and voice, or that of other users in your home, without exposing any of the data to the operating system.

That's important, because Siri AI now has access to more of your personal data, like your conversations in Messages, info from your Calendar invites and other bits of "personal context" from various apps. Of course, you have to opt-in to all of this by enabling Apple Intelligence on your iPhone or iPad, and you can limit which apps Siri can index data from. If you do decide to use the system, I can attest that it works well on the HomeView, though it's merely another vessel for the larger AI system.

During a lengthy conversation about lighthouses in North Carolina, Siri gave me information about visiting a variety of locations in the state. It provided information that I didn't specifically ask for, like which ones were still active, and got more comprehensive when my follow-up query was a bit vague. For example, when I asked "Which lighthouse is the easiest to get to?" Siri responded with both a list of ones you can park close to and highlighted the one that requires the least amount of climbing.

Siri was able to check my calendar to see if I was free to take the trip on a certain date and provided travel time from my house (including follow-up questions about the cities I'd pass through). I was finally able to stump the AI when I asked about "good hotels" near the Currituck Beach Lighthouse in Corolla, but even still, things felt more natural and conversational than interactions I've had with Siri in the past.

At times, Siri showed text answers on the HomeView in addition to reciting the info. When I asked about driving time or my schedule, a preview of the best route and the specific day on my calendar popped up respectively. What's more, I got a notification on my phone from the Home app letting me know Siri responded to a request for personal content. But it's important to remember that HomeView is just a conduit for Siri AI and its newly expanded abilities. These same things can be done on an iPhone or other compatible Apple devices, meaning you don't really have to spend $300 on a dedicated display for these tools.

One final note on security: Since the HomeView is heavily reliant on an iPhone or iPad, those devices must be nearby and recently unlocked (and enrolled in the shared home) for Siri to access personal data. The HomeView uses facial and voice recognition to know who's speaking, and to know when they walk away, so prying eyes can't see your texts if they rush over after you've been looking at Messages or other potentially sensitive apps. Guests or family members who don't have an iPhone or iPad can still use basic functions (control smart home devices, check weather, play music, etc), including asking Siri rudimentary questions.

When I first spent time with the HomeView last month, the feature I was most excited about was the recipe integration in Safari. I'm happy to report that feeling remains, for the most part, but there's one caveat that does impact the overall experience.

Let's back up for a second. On the HomeView, you can view a recipe link in Safari from your iPhone by holding it close to the display (just like you do to pass audio from your phone to a HomePod). Once the page loads, you'll see a cooking icon (a spoon and recipe card) in the bottom right corner. Tapping that will launch the HomeView's streamlined mode for the kitchen, which shows a recipe image with the dish's name, creator, cooking time and servings. There are two options underneath: Ingredients and Start Cooking.

The Ingredients option gives you a checklist-style collection of what you'll need for the recipe. If you need to add things to a shopping list in Reminders, Siri can do that for you. I had no trouble getting the AI assistant to add various ingredients to different shopping lists I have in that app (Costco, grocery store, etc). The HomeView will show its work by displaying the new entry in the Reminders app, and a Live Activities shortcut at the top allows you to quickly get back to the recipe with a tap.

Once you hit Start Cooking, the recipe's steps are divided into swipeable slides with inline shortcuts for any timers you need. Or, at least that's how it's supposed to be. While testing the HomeView, I pulled in recipes from my go-to cooks, and while some of them worked just like Apple advertised, others did not. This isn't a massive deal breaker, as you can still read the recipe's steps, but once you've experienced the correctly formatted version, it's hard to go back.

Going back means you'll get all of the steps on a single slide, requiring you to scroll instead of swipe. This is a long block of text, which can be overwhelming, especially compared to the short, easy-to-follow slides that alleviate some of the stress from following complicated instructions. And if you ever need to peek back at that ingredients list, the properly formatted versions have an always-present shortcut in the bottom right corner. To be clear, the formatting isn't an Apple problem; it's entirely up to the creator or publisher to present their recipes a certain way. I'm just pointing this all out to say Apple is advertising the ideal scenario, which is honestly an excellent experience, but you may not encounter it every time you cook with HomeView.

Another big piece of the HomeView puzzle is its ability to act as a smart home hub. With the Home app and devices compatible with HomeKit and Matter, this display becomes the command center for your cameras, thermostat, lighting and more. There's a Home dashboard that shows everything you've connected to the system. You can then create scenes and automations, or ask Siri to handle those for you.

We'll get to HomeView customization in a bit, but it's important to mention two Home faces here. The Home Modular face puts your favorite smart home devices on the same screen thanks to customizable widgets. These give you control over each connected gadget, including the ability to check a camera feed, without opening any apps on your phone. Of course, you can always ask Siri to do things for you by summoning the assistant on the HomeView. I'm sure Apple's AI is tired of adjusting my thermostat at this point, as that's what I've primarily been doing during my testing.

There's also a Home Camera face that's all video, all the time. Here, you can livestream up to nine feeds at once, giving you the ability to keep tabs on things wherever you have cameras positioned. If a visitor is detected, you can just tap on the HomeView to unlock a smart door lock (provided you have one of those, of course). We are a Blink household, though, and those aren't yet compatible with HomeKit, so I wasn't able to test any of Apple's camera features.

If you have an iCloud+ subscription, HomeView works with compatible security cameras (again, HomeKit and Matter) to detect people, pets, packages and vehicles. When any of those are seen, you'll get a notification and a live feed on the display. HomeView can also detect smoke or carbon monoxide alarms and send alerts to your iPhone, iPad or Apple Watch — even when you're away from home. That feature, known as Sound Recognition, is also available on the HomePod and HomePod mini.

The HomeView's speakers make it a solid option for music, podcasts and other audio entertainment. But, despite its crisp, vivid display, the device is not well-suited for watching videos. That's OK though, because right now, the only video app that's available on HomeView is Apple TV. And unless you like watching your shows within a thin band that sits in the middle of a square display, flanked by thick black bars on the top and bottom, you, too, will find the video experience on HomeView underwhelming. I would love to see Apple bring F1 multiview to this display though, putting two small alternate feeds below the main broadcast. Yes, they'd be tiny, but at least that's a better use of the space and eliminates the awkward black bars.

The HomeView runs on the same platform as CarPlay, which is why the various video streaming apps you have on your iPhone don't show up on this device. Apple says third-party developers are welcome to enable their apps for use on the HomeView, but doing so would also mean optimizing them for CarPlay. Only time will tell if developers are going to go through the extra steps of making that happen.

When it comes to audio-only content though, the HomeView is great. I'd classify the sound quality here as the midpoint between the HomePod mini and the larger HomePod. I've been reviewing the tabletop HomeView with a base, so I've enjoyed the added woofer and passive radiators, which balance out full-range drivers with extra bass. Across a variety of genres, bass is tight and punchy, and vocals cut through the mix clearly. The system never loses other instruments either, as you can easily pick up the guitars in Anberlin's "Gravity." Switch to something more chill, like Sam Barber's "If I May," and the acoustic instruments lay a base for the singer's unique vocals. Drums are more subdued here, and the HomeView does well to keep them from overpowering the mix.

I should also note the HomeView can get pretty darn loud. I measured 87dB with my Apple Watch standing right in front of the device. But I should also warn you that it's not a comfortable listening volume if you're standing close to the display. It might be nice for a party, but you'll likely stay around 50 percent or less for the most part.

Spatial Audio with Dolby Atmos is available on the HomeView for compatible music, movies and shows. However, given the compact stature of the device and its limited sonic prowess, you're more likely to notice the excellent overall clarity and even-handed tuning more than any dimensional effects. While watching an episode of Masters of the Air, for example, I found the sound perfectly fine, but wouldn't classify it as very immersive.

Apple included a few more entertainment-related conveniences on the HomeView that add utility. For one, it works with the HomePod, HomePod mini and AirPlay-compatible speakers for multiroom audio, which allows you to play the same thing everywhere or pick specific songs for each room. When listening to songs from Apple Music, you'll see animated album artwork if an artist has it, and lyrics are available with a single tap. And just like you can with your iPhone and HomePod speakers, you can handoff content from your phone to the HomeView just by placing your handset in front of the display.

The HomeView may look like an enlarged Apple Watch's screen, but the two devices also overlap in another area: faces. These screens are what you see when the HomeView is in what I like to call rest mode, when it's just sitting there not being actively used. You can have it just be a clock, as there are three faces just for that purpose, or display your calendar or the weather. There's also an animated Snoopy face that updates based on the time of day, weather, holidays and sporting events. Then there are the two Home faces that work with smart home devices that I already mentioned.

Those all have their purpose, but my two favorites are the Photos and Modular faces. The former makes the HomeView a digital picture frame and you can get specific about what shows up there. You can opt for it to cycle through photos of specific events (Memories), people, pets or cities, for example, but you can also set up Shared Albums that it will source from. If you do that last one, new photos will automatically appear every time they're added to the collection. The Modular face is what I've used most, as its customizable collection of widgets puts the calendar, date, time and weather in one spot. Of course, you can pick and choose what shows up here based on your needs or preferences, you're not forced to use the same combination I do.

The best thing about faces is that you can easily switch between them by swiping horizontally on one. A long press brings up the face customization interface where you're free to fine-tune things like color and widgets (where available) to your heart's content.

One feature that Apple has spent a lot of time showing off on the HomeView is FaceTime. The smart display has a 12-megapixel Center Stage camera in its top bezel, which means you can conduct video chats without reaching for your iPhone. Of course, the Center Stage software will keep you centered in the frame as you move around, which will be handy if you need to take a call from the kitchen while you're cooking, for example. You can start FaceTime calls on the HomeView, or you can hand them off from your iPhone. I'll also note that you can take voice calls from the HomeView, and in general, the device works well for both types of chats.

Find My is also available on the HomeView, allowing you to locate everything from your iPhone to your AirPods, or anything that has an AirTag attached to it. Of course, you can also use this to find family and friends who are sharing their location with you. The intercom feature from HomePods is on the HomeView as well, which comes in handy if you have multiple Apple speakers spread throughout your house. It also works with the iPhone, iPad and Apple Watch, plus you can send and receive intercom messages from anywhere — not just inside your home.

Lastly, Live Activities displays real-time info for a variety of tasks on HomeView. Like it does on your iPhone, the feature puts your progress at the top of the display on a floating, tappable button. Live Activities on the HomeView are a bit limited though, only capable of showing active timers/alarms, FaceTime calls, Safari media playback and Now Playing controls for Apple Music or Apple TV. I found it also surfaced audio controls for my go-to podcast app Pocket Casts as well. One thing that doesn't show up there that I wish it did is Live Activities from the Sports app. It would be great if the live scores for my favorite teams or an update on who's leading the F1 grand prix were available at a glance.

Apple is years behind the competition when it comes to smart displays. The company's two main rivals in this category, Amazon and Google, have been honing their products since 2017 and 2018 respectively. The premise has always been similar to Apple's: a combination display and speaker that's a conduit for each company's virtual assistant — or in 2026 terms, each company's AI.

Amazon's current Echo Show lineup has six options, ranging from 5 inches up to 21 inches, with the largest models ditching the speaker base for a wall mount or stand. The most affordable, the Echo Show 5, is $100. Google's current slate of smart displays is much more modest, with only the aging second-generation Nest Hub and Nest Hub Max as the available options. Those are going for $100 and $229 respectively, and only the pricier of the two has a built-in camera for video calls.

Apple may have lagged behind the competition on introducing a smart display, but the HomeView checks a lot of boxes that make it a very powerful option for iPhone users. It's also more elegantly designed than all of its main competitors. The ability to automatically distinguish between different family members, and for Siri to pull in personalized context for each person, makes this a useful home hub. When you factor in the cooking assistance, smart home compatibility and FaceTime chops, the HomeView can easily become the command center for any household. Just be sure to keep your iPhone (or iPad) handy if you want to make the most of it.

#324. Netflix releases new trailer for Ray Gunn

“It’s hard being the only honest players in a crooked game.”

We caught the first trailer for the upcoming Netflix animated film Ray Gunn in September and were charmed by director Brad Bird’s blend of a Jetsons-like future with classic 1930s noir detective thrillers. Bird has described it as a mashup of The Maltese Falcon and Buck Rogers, and the latest trailer definitely gives off that energy.

As previously reported, Bird has directed some of our all-time favorite animated films: The Iron Giant (1999), The Incredibles (2004), and Ratatouille (2007), with the latter two snagging well-deserved Oscar wins. He’s been kicking around the idea for Ray Gunn for the last 30 years, working on his many other film projects while the Ray Gunn script—co-written by Matthew Robbins, who also co-wrote 1987’s Batteries Not Included with Bird—“stayed in a filing cabinet at Warner Brothers,” the director said earlier this year.

The film’s inspiration came when Bird heard the opening of the B-52s’ “Planet Claire” (a personal favorite that also provides the soundtrack to the new trailer) and initially thought it was a cover of Henry Mancini’s theme music from the classic TV show Peter Gunn. Bird is a longtime fan of film noir and hard-boiled detectives, and he came up with the idea of a futuristic detective named Ray Gunn (a play on “raygun,” of course). “It’s film noir and a hard-boiled detective story, but it’s got a lot more comedy and a lot more action than the average film noir,” said Bird.

Per the official premise: “In Metropia, a gigantic city in an alternate future as seen from 1939, private eye Raymond Gunn (voiced by Sam Rockwell) is drawn into a case involving aliens, murder, and a multimedia star named Venus Nova (voiced by Scarlett Johansson).”

Unlike Bird’s Pixar fare, Ray Gunn targets a slightly older demographic with more mature themes.

In addition to Rockwell and Johansson, the all-star voice cast includes Tom Waits as Ray’s one-eyed alien colleague, Eyera (pronounced Ira); Matt Berry as alien mob boss Francis “Sluggy” Xavier; Bobby Cannavale as Venus’ husband, who hires Ray to spy on his wife; Alan Tudyk as Venus’ manager, Barry Vogel; Michael Chernus as a studio fixer named Sam; and Patton Oswalt as a hologram. Bird provides the voices for Xavier’s alien henchmen, Gunsol and Musker. Kathryn Hunter, John Ratzenberger, and Jamie Costa will also voice as-yet-undisclosed characters.

Ray Gunn releases on Netflix on December 18, 2026—the same day both Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part 3 hit theaters, so it will be alternative family fare for those disinclined to brave the holiday theatrical crowds. The film will also screen in select theaters on December 4, 2026.

#323. The first batch of Android XR smartglasses made me rethink my stance on specs without displays

I still want models with built-in screens, but Google’s first run of intelligent eyewear has a lot to offer.

The first device powered by Android XR came out last year when Samsung released the Galaxy XR headset. But this fall, Google is taking its augmented reality platform in a different direction with the launch of intelligent eyewear from partners such as Warby Parker and Gentle Monster. This gives Google its own version of lightweight smartglasses to rival competitors like the third-gen Ray-Ban Meta line, and after testing them out, it made me reconsider my feelings about smart specs without built-in displays.

As this first generation of intelligent eyewear is a bit of a departure from previous Android XR devices, I think it's important to get some of the basics out of the way first. For this family of specs, Google is in charge of the OS while Samsung is handling much of the tech — including things like the cameras, mics, touch sensors and more. All of that then gets handed over to Warby Parker and Gentle Monster, who are in charge of the glasses design, lenses, general sales and customer support.

Right off the bat, this seems like a great division of responsibilities because it allows each company to do what they're best at. Google handles the software, Samsung takes care of the hardware and the real glasses makers ensure you have good-looking frames with the right prescriptions (you can even opt for transition/photochromic lenses). And right out of the gate, it feels like there's already a lot of interesting styles and color combos to choose from, regardless if you're a bit more fashion-forward or if you, like me, prefer a more traditional frame.

Another important thing to note is that while Gentle Monster's eyewear will be a touch more expensive than Warby Parker's models ($449 and $395, respectively), they will have the same general tech specs. Those include the same 2GB of RAM and 64GB of storage, a 12MP camera (that also handles up to 3K video), stereo speakers, far-field mics and a built-in touch sensor. Battery life is also the same across the board with around nine hours of use on a single charge, while the case for the glasses stores enough juice for multiple full charges.

Furthermore, you won't be required to have a paid Gemini plan to use any of the features of the glasses; it's all free. These glasses will even work with both Android devices and iPhones with feature parity across both OSes. That said, there won't be any options with a built-in display, which means Google won't have a true rival to the Meta Ray-Ban Display glasses, at least not yet.

When it comes to features, it's no surprise that these glasses have a lot of overlap with the Ray-Ban Meta specs. There's a physical button near the right temple for capturing images: A short press takes a picture while a long press records videos. There's also a touch sensor built into the right arm for doing stuff like controlling volume, pausing audio or dismissing Gemini. And of course, the glasses are connected to your phone wirelessly (either Bluetooth or Wi-Fi depending on the situation) so you have access to streaming music, notifications, messages, supported apps and more. However, the real lynchpin of the entire platform is Google's AI, which can be easily summoned via voice controls or by performing a long press on the touch panel.

Now I'll admit that I don't interact with Gemini all that often when I'm using a phone or a laptop. When I already have access to a screen, a keyboard and all sorts of dedicated apps, so I find I can often perform tasks faster manually than asking an AI assistant to do them. But when wearing smartglasses, it's a whole different story. If you want to respond to a text, get turn-by-turn directions or start some tunes, you can do all that hands-free with simple voice commands while keeping your phone in your pocket. Granted, that is sort of table stakes for smartglasses these days, but being able to walk around with your head up while dodging traffic or other pedestrians can't be understated.

But it goes deeper, because thanks to Gemini's intelligence and persistent memory between conversations, the AI in your glasses has a much deeper understanding of how to help you. Instead of constantly having to say a wake word all the time, you can have more natural back and forth dialogue. This allows you to get updates on your events, make adjustments to questions and pick up on previous queries even when they happen throughout the course of your day (you can specify how long you want Gemini to remember conversations).

Where things get really interesting is the combination of computer vision and AI, which allowed me to do things like pick up a physical invite card and simply ask Gemini to add it to my calendar. No need to grab my phone, open up an app and add details like the time, date and location myself. I was also able to do things like simply look at a bunch of different ingredients and ask Gemini to make a smoothie from what was in front of me. It can even do rough estimates for how much of a certain food has been added, so if you're not worried about being super precise, you can leave your measuring cups in a drawer. And if you run out of certain foods, you can easily tell Gemini to add something to your grocery list.

Then there are other features like live translation, which feels kind of magical. The glasses are designed so that the mics are directed in a cone-shaped space in front of you, so that even in a room with other conversations going on, the glasses don't get confused. Not only does this let you switch speakers naturally just by looking at someone else, Gemini will even adjust the output of a translation to sound similar to the original speaker. This allows you to follow along in real-time almost as well as someone who speaks the language naturally.

That said, the thing that impressed me the most about these Android XR glasses is the platform itself. Compared to some rivals where a lot of features feel like a work in progress, Google's OS feels much more mature. This includes little touches like dedicated sounds when Gemini accesses your camera, when the glasses take a photo or video, when Gemini leverages external AI and more. Google has done a pretty good job of translating a lot of the most important features you use on your phone to a medium without a screen.

The one caveat to all this is that you do have to buy into talking to and relying rather heavily on Gemini. I'm still not sure I'm 100 percent on board with that, but at least for me, these glasses make a better argument for always-on AI support than almost any other class of device I've used. So while I'm still waiting for companies to deliver streamlined specs with built-in displays, these audio-first options feel like much more than a stepping stone towards that. They're surprisingly full-featured, and I don't think it's a stretch to say that compared to rival assistants like Siri, Muse and others, Gemini is the smartest of the bunch. So even if you primarily want these for playing music, keeping tabs on your schedule and getting occasional help from AI, Google's first batch of Android XR-powered intelligent eyewear feels like it's already jumped to the front of the pack.

Pre-orders for the new family of Android XR intelligent eyewear from both Warby Parker and Gentle Monster begin today with official sales slated for December 10.

#320. As Fresh Air Becomes a Luxury, Many Indonesians Turn on Their Leader

In Indonesia, support for President Prabowo Subianto has collapsed as his government has failed to contain raging wildfires, compounding quality-of-life woes.

After more than four months of living under the shroud of wildfire haze in Indonesian Borneo and keeping his young sons indoors, Kurniawan rues voting for President Prabowo Subianto.

Far to the west, on the island of Sumatra, Rama has the same regret. He has developed chest pain and breathing difficulties due to the bad air.

“The situation has gotten so severe, yet, instead of empathy, the government is turning a blind eye to our suffering,” said Rama, who like other voters interviewed for this article asked to be identified only by part of his name to speak freely about the country’s leader.

The haze has spread hundreds of miles across national borders to Malaysia, Singapore and the Philippines, raising fears of a regional health crisis. And for millions of Indonesians, particularly in Borneo and Sumatra, it has compounded cost of living woes by making fresh air a luxury.

#319. Microsoft global outage: What the company has said so far

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 5:39 p.m. ET — As of this writing, the global Microsoft outage remains ongoing.

Updated on Thursday, Nov. 12 at 3:55 p.m. ET — We've updated this story with the latest statements from Microsoft.

Microsoft is having a really bad Thursday.

Starting in the early afternoon, Microsoft 365 enterprise software products experienced a major outage, which is ongoing at the time of publication. On the platform DownDetector, more than 100,000 users reported problems with Outlook alone.

The company has posted regular updates about the situation, both on social media and on its website.

On the company's official status page (which also went offline briefly, according to multiple Mashable reporters), Microsoft said that Exchange Online, Teams, SharePoint Online, Copilot 365, and Defender XDR were all experiencing issues. The status page also indicated that the outage is only affecting Microsoft's enterprise software, Microsoft 365, not its consumer products.

Mashable has reached out to Microsoft for comment, but the company hadn't responded at the time of publication.

In an initial update posted at 12:54 p.m. ET to X, the company said that customers were having problems with Exchange Online mailboxes "in the Americas." Exchange Online is a cloud-based tool that provides back-end support for Microsoft Outlook.

However, by 1:07 p.m. ET, the company acknowledged the problem was more widespread, affecting more services and regions. "While we're working to mitigate impact, we've confirmed the issue may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region," the company posted.

The Microsoft Status page was then updated with a notification that stated, "User impact: Users may be unable to connect to multiple Microsoft 365 services." The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

While a root cause has yet to be determined, Microsoft has at least shared a brief technical explanation for what's going on.

"We've determined that the elevated load isn't driven by an overall increase in traffic, but by a large number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable, which is shifting load onto the remaining infrastructure," Microsoft said on its status page, as well as in a thread on X. "We're investigating what's causing these servers to become unavailable while we work to safely return them to service."

Later on in the X thread, Microsoft said it had not identified any recent change that correlates with the outage, but "the failed health checks may originate from a specific component that's responsible for processing server health." In the meantime, Microsoft has worked to prevent more servers from being impacted while it also tries to resuscitate the ones that are down.

Shortly before 4 p.m. ET, the Microsoft status page received another update, stating that it is still troubleshooting and restarting servers.

"While validating the targeted change, we found that many of the affected servers have become unresponsive, which is preventing us from confirming whether the change is effective," Microsoft said. "We're restarting these servers so they can come back online with the change in place, and we're applying the same approach to additional affected portions of our infrastructure to validate the results more broadly."

By the evening, Microsoft's updates turned slightly more optimistic, as the company stated that some servers had already come back online. #318. Google really needs you to know it cares about smart glasses privacy

Public trust in smart glasses is very important for selling lots of smart glasses.

The world has changed in the ten years since Snap first launched its camera-equipped Spectacles. Not only has the smart glasses market grown, the number of people sharing videos online has also jumped significantly. Nowadays, thanks to the rise of platforms like TikTok and Reels, anyone can be an influencer. And the possibility of innocent passersby inadvertently being included in a viral clip is far higher than it was.

It is in this climate that Google launches its intelligent eyewear devices today. This year has already seen Meta catch heat for what people called its "pervert glasses" or "creep glasses." In addition to making people feel uncomfortable about being spied on or filmed without their consent, the controversy has also discouraged existing users of those glasses from continuing to wear them, not to mention possibly putting off potential buyers.

Let's start with what's happening on the glasses. It's basically table stakes at this point, but they all have an LED light on them to indicate when someone is taking a picture or recording a video. The light, which sits on the left side of the frame while the camera is next to the right eye, has sensors to detect if it's covered or tampered with and will prevent the capture of pictures or video.

"Unfortunately, some of the glasses that have launched in the past launched without any kind of detection for those things, and that broke trust." Payne said. "And that's a problem," he added. He also said Google's methods are "pretty effective," though that's something that we'll have to test for ourselves to verify.

And just as there are bad actors who will continue to try to break security protections for any safe in the world or online, there will be people attempting to get around Google's anti-tampering measures here. Payne said "there will always be ways around what we build, and then we'll try to fix those holes," and that he expects there will be an ongoing back-and-forth. "But nonetheless, we're very happy with where we're starting on this one."

One of the main functions of Google's smart glasses is the ability for Gemini AI to use the camera to see what the wearer is looking at and provide more relevant information. During this process, the company says that the camera visuals won't be stored in your Gemini history. When there are faces in view, Google says "your glasses are also designed to automatically redact bystanders' faces before Gemini processes the visuals."

The camera stream also won't be used in AI training or ad targeting, and Google says any data that is sent off the glasses for processing, say in the cloud or on your phone, is encrypted for transit. It's also promising to bring the Private AI Compute processing platform to glasses, enabling these new devices to use the "hardware-secured sealed cloud environment" for greater processing.

While the camera may be the most obvious recording sensor on these devices, they also have four microphones onboard to allow you to speak to Gemini or get translations for people around you. Google uses beamforming to help focus on the individuals you're looking at when deciding whose speech to translate, and the company also said it "designed Gemini to focus on your voice by default, and not other voices around you, using directional microphones on the glasses."

Payne said that the microphones will filter out "all the world audio, as we call it, the bystander audio," It helps prevent accidental recording of people in your vicinity, but also improves Gemini's effectiveness by making sure it doesn't confuse someone else's heated argument as your query.

Of course, to make the glasses more useful, you'll have to grant it access to your apps and personal information. The intelligent eyewear can work with apps like Messages, Keep or YouTube Music, for instance, to send replies or create lists for you. Like on most phones, you'll have to grant permission to specific apps before they can access the camera on your glasses, and even after you do, you can still add or revoke access for every app.

Google also said you can view your past interactions with Gemini, delete individual chats or all of them. You'll also be able to decide if your conversations are used to train AI. And to make sure there's no confusion over whether you left the glasses on or off, and if the microphones or camera might still be tuning in, all the new eyewear have a power switch with a clear red indicator to tell you when they've been shut down.

Most intriguing are the measures Google is implementing across its entire ecosystem. The company said it has protections in place "across Google apps and services to deter misuse of the glasses," and they're pretty far-reaching.

For one thing, Google Shopping and Google Ads will "ban the sale and advertising of stickers or accessories designed to hide recording lights."

The company is also working to cull the demand for videos of a harassing or creepy nature. Payne said that one of the problems Google has seen is a small number of people "who use glasses to take creepy videos."

YouTube will "prohibit content that depicts things like harassment and unwanted sexualization. Any video uploaded to YouTube — whether it's recorded on a phone, a camera, or glasses — has to follow these policies, and videos that don't are removed."

There are also considerations for apps that request permission to use the cameras on these devices. "There's multiple approval layers if an app wants to use the camera," Payne said. "And there's actually some use cases that we're just blanket cutting off."

To that end, Google said that the Play Store and API policies will "prohibit apps from using the camera for social live-streaming or real-time facial identification, and from changing the behavior of the outer indicator light." This live-streaming restriction also applies to YouTube.

Of course, since the eyewear will be compatible with iOS devices as well, it's possible that someone finds an app of that nature in the App Store and uses it. And though Google may ban the advertisement or sale of physical goods that can be used to hide the LED indicator, people could very easily procure one on Amazon or Temu. There's more that needs to be done against this problem, and Google alone cannot cover all bases.

Sure, there's the honor system approach that most modern recording devices already use. Things like requiring an 18+ Google account to activate an intelligent eyewear device and asking users to agree to not recording others without permission or in sensitive places are in place here as well.

But the responsibility of prevention of misuse doesn't just fall to the user or Google. There needs to be greater collaboration across the industry for these measures to be effective. Payne said that Google is sharing these statements now in an attempt to set an example and send a message. "We hope that it resonates with people, and we hope that as a byproduct of that, some others in this space say, 'You know what? We should probably tighten up some of our policies.'"

If it's able to get the attention and cooperation of other players in the space, Google stands to benefit. "We think that that will then earn a lot of trust back in the category, then we can focus a lot on how cool and valuable these are."

#317. Google faces calls for AI moratoriums in schools after Gemini pilot backlash

Families confront Google after teen’s lawsuit labels Gemini a “suicide coach.”

CHICAGO—Families joined protests in seven cities nationwide on Wednesday, rallying against what they see as Google’s bid to entrench its AI models in schools, turn kids of all ages into Gemini guinea pigs, and hook them on AI.

The backlash follows a Google statement last week, confirming that there will be no way for students to use Google Search without AI. While Google claimed it was giving schools “more choice, not less”—by turning off Search, Gemini, and Gemini Notebook by default—it would not provide an option that allowed Search without AI. For students, the only way to avoid interacting with AI was to remember to add “-ai” to every query, parents worried.

In Chicago, many families showed up to protest Google, despite cold, rainy weather. Waving signs, dozens of protesters marched up to Google’s downtown office as workers seemed to be leaving for the day, shouting chants like “Hey Google, nice try, we don’t want your Gemini” and “Hey, Google, leave our kids alone!”

Called to action by a nonprofit grassroots advocacy group, the Illinois Families for Public Schools (ILFPS), some parents and students had previously won a major AI fight. In August, the group pressured Chicago Public Schools (CPS) to pull out of a Gemini pilot that would have roped in all high school students districtwide—without consulting parents. And now, ILFPS is demanding a moratorium on AI until risks to students are better understood and CPS can draft a policy to protect students from potential learning setbacks or more harmful outcomes from engaging Google’s AI bots. If the group wins, it will join cities like Los Angeles and New York City in cautiously banning AI in schools.

Most alarming to parents, Google now faces a lawsuit from a 13-year-old student in Durham, North Carolina named “Emma Doe.” As Emma has alleged, she was a happy, well-adjusted, straight-A student when she started using Gemini on her school-issued Chromebook. Emma’s parents had no idea she was using Gemini, as the lawsuit alleged that Google “deliberately” kept them in the dark while driving Emma into so-called “AI psychosis.” Over time, normal school-related interactions with the chatbot led to more intense chats, and ultimately the chatbot allegedly coached Emma to plan her own suicide and funeral. The school warned her parents and averted the danger after an internal system flagged a chat labeled “Funeral,” but Emma’s story stoked fears of parents joining protests not just in Durham, but also in Boston; Boulder, Colorado; Chicago; Los Angeles; New York City; and Portland, Oregon.

Emma’s lawsuit accuses Google of exploiting its dominant position in education technology and “sneaking” Gemini into Chromebooks, while falsely marketing them as safe.

“As a direct consequence of Google’s choices, Emma was baited by its defectively designed products into repeated, unmonitored, sycophantic AI chatbot interactions on her school Chromebook without her parents ever knowing. And, but for a fortunate turn of events, Google’s AI may well have manipulated Emma into taking her own life,” the lawsuit alleged. “Google must be held to account for treating the lives of children as disposable in service of its bottom line.”

Google has promised to “continue to engage deeply with educators to ensure we are giving schools more choice, more controls, and improved Search for learning.” However, Google’s statements so far attempting to address parents’ concerns focus exclusively on documented harms to learning. On that front, Google claims that it’s solving the problem of AI eroding students’ critical thinking skills by launching yet another pilot for students. In this pilot, students using AI Mode and AI Overviews will be offered a “step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don’t get quick answers to schoolwork,” Google said.

Cassie Creswell, executive director of ILFPS, told Ars that she’s been an advocate monitoring edtech issues for more than a decade. And even she was blindsided by Google’s AI moves.

“I am aware of AI Overviews when you search, which have a whole slew of problems, but I did not know that you really actually just have an interface where you can interact with Gemini in the chatbot way on everyone’s Chromebook still in Chicago public schools,” Creswell said. “And that’s so disturbing.”

She acknowledged that kids encounter AI on their phones and shared that her daughter said her friends like using ChatGPT better than Gemini. But she said that the fact that some kids use AI on personal devices doesn’t mean that schools should be encouraging adoption at young ages when kids’ brains are still developing, and risks remain largely unknown.

“There’s all kinds of dangerous things out in the world, but we don’t have our school district handing it to kids at their desk,” Creswell said.

Meanwhile, schools seem to be part of the transparency problem.

Creswell said that currently, the Chicago school district “is telling us their official line is that kids don’t have access to generative AI tools and that we don’t need a district-level policy on generative AI because they have guidebooks.” However, she’s reviewed Google’s CPS contracts, and they suggest that kids are interacting with generative AI and frontier models. The ongoing confusion has led parents to demand “a real policy” that the CPS “board votes on and that has the input of teachers, parents, students, and experts who are not being funded by the tech industry.”

Creswell told Ars that parents are particularly upset that Google is not transparent about how it processes and uses student data. She’s exhaustively researched Google contracts through Freedom of Information Act requests and told Ars that dozens of agreements she reviewed offer no indication of how CPS partners like Google—as well as Anthropic and OpenAI—are handling student data. Although she understands that “it’s probably just an honest statement of fact” that Google has built AI inextricably into Search, parents have a right to know how their children’s data is ingested into Google’s models, she said.

“Any sort of transparency on what is actually happening in terms of the data that they collect and what they do with it, that is absolutely missing,” Creswell said. And “it’s very unclear what data is being saved.”

To Creswell and other advocates, it seems likely that kids’ data is collected no matter what, since “even if you do ‘-AI,’ and you don’t get a text summary, your search data and what’s being returned is all basically inside the generative AI model.” Although Illinois law only allows commercial use of de-identified data, parents think Google should clearly explain what steps it takes to ensure data can’t be recombined to re-identify kids.

Ars reached out to Google to see if the company could clarify if Creswell’s read is accurate or if there’s any meaningful distinction between adding “-AI” to an individual query versus building a button that would automatically add “-AI” to every query. Google did not clarify, instead emphasizing that schools will control whether students can access the Gemini app, Gemini Notebook, and Search, with options to toggle each off individually, but no option to use Search without AI.

Currently, that policy specifies that “we receive customer data through the Core Services and process it according to the school’s (the customer’s) instructions. This customer data includes anything submitted, stored, sent, or received through Core Services by you or your school.” Google collects that data on that activity, as well as account information “like name, email address and password” and location information, the policy said.

The policy didn’t discuss Gemini but noted that data from using core services helps maintain services, improve personalization, and increase security. And “the data we collect in the Additional Services is used across our services to deliver, maintain, and improve our services; develop new services; provide personalized services; measure performance; communicate with you; and protect Google, our users, and the public.”

To parents, the policy provides no reassurance that their kids’ data isn’t part of Google’s plan to dominate the AI industry.

“We should not have any child data being collected in a school setting being used for commercial purposes,” Creswell said.

In Chicago, a recently re-elected CPS school board member, Karen Zaccor, is pushing for an AI moratorium in schools after talking with city and community colleges about an allegedly noticeable decline in reading, math, and writing skills from students graduating from CPS schools. In Emma’s case, Google allegedly suppressed chat logs, and the school withheld safety logs, frustrating parents who already viewed AI as more harmful than beneficial in education.

At the Google protest Wednesday, parents waved signs that threatened to eject Google from schools, with slogans like “No Google + No AI = Win/Win.” Many kids used favorite cartoon characters from shows like Beavis and Butthead and South Park to slam Google’s seemingly cynical approach to introducing AI in schools. One young girl wearing pink rain boots held up a cardboard sign, declaring in colorful markers, “I like to read!!”

At its peak, about 60 people stood outside Google’s downtown Chicago office, enduring the rain and listening to teachers and students who oppose reckless AI adoption.

One particularly moving speech came from a CPS student, Reese Mackey, who spoke for students. She challenged Google to more carefully weigh the long-term harms of aggressively pushing AI onto students by claiming they’ll be left behind and struggle to find jobs without AI skills. Creswell told Ars the opposite seems true, as Emma Doe’s lawsuit pointed to research branding Gemini as “the most dangerous marketed for use by youth” and “confirming the existence of AI-induced psychosis.”

“What about the future of our youth?” Mackey said. “What about the future of our minds?”

#314. Jensen Huang is out as NVIDIA CEO

He has held the position since the company's founding.

On Thursday, NVIDIA's board of directors said that Huang had resigned his position as president and chief executive of the company, effective immediately. Huang also gave up his board seat. NVIDIA named Colette Kress, the company's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as interim CEO.

"Kress will continue to serve as Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer while serving as Interim Chief Executive Officer until a permanent successor has been named," NVIDIA said. "The Board is initiating a search for a permanent Chief Executive Officer and will consider internal and external candidates."

As part of the shakeup, NVIDIA also appointed its first-ever chair of the board in Harvey Jones, who previously served as CEO and executive chairman of electronic design automation company Synopsys. "Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position," Jones said in a statement. "She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA's strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption."

Huang's departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The board said at the time it had "no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised." The FBI is also investigating, according to President Donald Trump.

The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him, and that the two were seen "being affectionate" at a hotel during a company conference this year. A bystander who reportedly recorded the interaction was offered a nondisclosure agreement by Huang's security detail, according to the report.

Huang's downfall is a stunning turn for the company, which has become a linchpin for the AI industry. He has been CEO since co-founding the company in 1993. Since then, he has transformed NVIDIA from a 3D graphics company to an AI powerhouse worth more than $4 trillion.

As NVIDIA's stock price has exploded in recent years Huang has become an increasingly public face of the AI boom. He often appeared at events — usually wearing his signature black leather jacket — with fellow tech CEOs like Mark Zuckerberg and Satya Nadella. At a time when high profile AI researchers are raising the alarm about superintelligence, Huang has had a much more positive view on the technology. He argued against increased regulation of the industry and has said America should not restrict China's access to US-made chips.

Huang has frequently been seen with President Trump, and was one of several tech leaders to accompany him on a recent trip to China. Asked about recent revelations, the president said he believed Huang was "an honorable guy" and that he "probably has an excuse for it."

In a memo to NVIDIA employees earlier this week in which he apologized for his actions, Huang wrote that leading the company was "the privilege of my life" and that he was "committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security." He added that he was "fully committed to NVIDIA" and would "work to earn back your confidence." But the CEO who once seemed untouchable won't get the chance to do so.

#313. Why U.S. Business Deals Are Unlikely to Get Putin to Give Any Ground

President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia has shown a willingness to endure a high price to get what he wants in Ukraine, and he sees little reason to stop fighting now, analysts say.

Reporting from Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates

For a year and a half, as the United States engaged Russia in on-again, off-again peace talks, the Trump administration held off on cutting any business deals with Moscow, insisting that the Kremlin first take concrete steps toward ending the war in Ukraine.

That all changed this fall. President Trump brokered a deal to waive sanctions and import Russian diesel ahead of the midterm elections. U.S. officials held talks on a range of possible deals, including the sale of the international assets of the Russian energy giant Lukoil and the resumption of Russian gas exports to Europe.

U.S. officials argued that the frenzy of potential deal-making could build good will and help compel the Kremlin to stop fighting. But the inducements have done little to curb President Vladimir V. Putin’s central demands on the war, which include placing limits on Ukraine’s defenses and alliances and broadly reordering the security architecture in Europe.

Those demands are at the center of peace talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials that Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, Mr. Trump’s envoys, are convening on Tuesday and Wednesday in the United Arab Emirates. The talks, the first since February involving all three countries, will address thorny topics like how to put in place an eventual cease-fire and whether a compromise can be reached on territory, U.S. officials said.

Top security advisers have flown in from European countries and NATO as momentum builds for Europe to become more directly involved in the peace process. The European officials will meet with the Americans and the Ukrainians, with a view toward fostering more direct discussions between European and Russian leaders in the future, the U.S. officials said.

But to pull Mr. Putin toward compromise, it is going to take a lot more than business deals, analysts say.
