#312. Ukraine says it can’t stop Russian strikes because Starshield is limited again

Report: Ukraine suspects speeds limits are intended to help Russia in peace talks.

Ukraine President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said today that Starshield has again been disabled on fast-moving drones in Ukrainian territory occupied by Russian forces, and that the US has provided “very little” information about the return of the restriction.

“We have detected speed restrictions on Starshield on our Ukrainian territory again, in the temporarily occupied parts of Ukraine,” Zelenskyy said in a speech at the 9th European Political Community Summit in Ireland. “The speed limit is back at 161 kilometers per hour—that’s 100 miles per hour.”

Zelenskyy’s allegation is that Starshield—the military variant of SpaceX’s Starlink satellite service—stops working on Ukrainian military drones when they travel above 161 kilometers per hour.

SpaceX today denied Zelenskyy’s allegation. “This is false. There has been no change,” SpaceX’s media relations department told Ars today when asked if SpaceX has any response to Zelenskyy’s statement that Starshield is being restricted again.

SpaceX has received multiple Pentagon contracts to give Ukraine access to the military-focused Starshield. SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said last month that any decisions on Starshield restrictions are made by the US government. The Pentagon inspector general’s office has since declined a request from US senators to investigate who ordered the restrictions.

Ukraine officials reportedly said today that they believe the limit was reinstated to put pressure on Ukraine in the latest round of peace talks with Russia. Zelenskyy said in today’s speech that without satellite connectivity on its drones, Ukraine cannot intercept Russian drones that fly in from the occupied parts of Ukraine.

It has often been unclear whether US and Ukrainian government officials discussing the restrictions were referring to Starlink, the civilian service that isn’t supposed to be used for offensive military purposes, or Starshield, which relies on the same technology but is intended for military use. But today, Zelenskyy clearly stated that the current restrictions affect Starshield.

“This directly affects our ability to intercept Russian jet-powered Shahed drones,” Zelenskyy said. “These drones come from those [occupied] areas, and because of the speed restrictions, we simply cannot catch up with them in time. Near the front line, we cannot use our aircraft. Only drone interceptors can operate there. And without the speed we need, shooting down these Shaheds becomes much harder. This means more Russian strikes get through, and more of our people are killed, our civilians are killed, by Russia.”

Ukraine officials reported the first round of restrictions about a month ago, but the limits were removed by October 24. Zelenskyy said today that Ukraine doesn’t know why the limit is in place again.

“We are trying to get the details from the American side, but so far we know very little,” Zelenskyy said. “But some things are very clear. If someone in America is making decisions that basically treat Ukrainian territory as if it is not Ukrainian, then this tells us where things are going. We need to be clear and never recognize Russia’s occupation of Ukrainian territory in any way.”

A Financial Times article today described Ukrainian officials’ allegations that the Starshield limit was put back in place to influence peace talks:

Senior Ukrainian officials told the FT they believed the speed restrictions were imposed in an effort to pressure Kyiv during the latest peace talks with Russia, which just concluded in Abu Dhabi. US special envoy Steve Witkoff and Donald Trump’s son-in-law, Jared Kushner, were in Moscow on Thursday to meet Russian President Vladimir Putin, after which they were set to travel to Ireland to meet Zelenskyy. One senior Ukrainian official said it appeared that Washington wanted to strengthen Russia’s position in the talks.

“These are not random errors, but systematic speed restrictions,” Zelenskyy said. “For us, this primarily means a significant complication in intercepting Russian jet-powered drones.”

Musk last month accused Ukraine of using the Starlink commercial service for offensive drone strikes, which he said violates the terms of service. A Ukraine official denied Musk’s accusation and said previous limits imposed by SpaceX already prevented Ukraine’s military from using the commercial Starlink service in the way Musk described.

As for the Starshield military variant, Musk has said that “Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the US government. Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS.” Trump denied making the decision to limit Ukraine’s satellite connectivity, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio said the administration had “no foreknowledge” of the slowdown.

We contacted the White House today and will update this article if we get a response. European Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier said that restrictions on Ukraine’s use of Starshield are unacceptable.

“It is not the first time that Ukraine is facing Starshield disruptions. These reports are deeply concerning and, if confirmed, unacceptable,” Regnier said at a press conference. “SpaceX must have a consistent approach in sharing its services with Ukraine. Starshield services must be restored urgently for Ukraine to keep its soldiers connected, to protect its citizens, and to defend its territory.”

#308. Google rolls out Gemini 4 Hydrogen, finally replacing 3.8 Flash

Gemini 4 Hydrogen isn’t getting a slow, security-conscious release like Carbon and Argon.

Google is continuing its elemental AI naming scheme with the new Gemini 4 Hydrogen. These names are less descriptive than the Pro/Flash monikers of earlier releases, but Hydrogen’s purpose is clear: It replaces the faster, more economical Flash models most people in Google’s ecosystem use.

With Google’s recent Argon and Carbon releases, the rollout was staged and began with cybersecurity researchers. Even when general availability began, Google restricted access to users paying for its most expensive AI subscription. The limits for Hydrogen are much more relaxed, with access beginning immediately. This model has replaced Gemini 3.8 Flash across the company’s products.

Google claims that Hydrogen represents a major improvement versus the previous Flash model. It runs in one of four thinking levels (low, medium, high, and the new xhigh), and even in high mode, it’s 2.5 times faster than Flash 3.8. Plus, it reduces “fabricated claims” by more than 5x, according to Google’s analysis. Users will also see gains in code generation, with Hydrogen more than tripling 3.8 Flash’s score in Terminal-Bench. In some cases, Hydrogen even trades blows with Argon. It managed a score of 54 in the popular Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index with xhigh mode versus 50 for Argon.

Google believes Hydrogen will be a key part of how developers work with agentic coding tools. While it still positions Argon and Carbon as the best for long-horizon tasks and security, Hydrogen’s speed makes it ideal for generating simpler code and running tests without blowing through token budgets.

Hydrogen is also being integrated with Google Search, which the company says is the biggest update to its AI-powered search tools ever. The new model should better handle long, multipart questions, and the responses in both AI Overviews and AI Mode will have more visual elements. The company also claims Hydrogen’s fan-out search has been tuned to link to more sources, but we’ve heard that from Google before.

#304. Zelenskyy says Starshield has been slowed again, endangering Ukraine's defense

It comes three weeks after Ukraine’s speeds were restored following the last throttling incident.

As media headlines focus on Jensen Huang's resignation and the midterms fallout, you could almost forget that Ukraine is still fighting for its life — with all of Europe nervously watching. According to President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, his job just got harder again. The country's access to Starshield, the military version of Starlink, has been hit with "systematic speed restrictions" for several days. And it just so happens to be while Trump's envoys are in Moscow for talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Zelenskyy posted on X that the speed limits are being imposed along the war's entire front line and in Russian-occupied territory. He said any terminal moving faster than 161 km/h (100 mph) loses its connection to the satellite network. Following briefings from his defense minister and top commander, Zelenskyy said Ukraine's military had investigated and ruled out random errors.

"Unfortunately, this is helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine," Zelenskyy said. "We are trying to establish all the circumstances with the American side."

Ukraine's interceptor drones rely on Starshield to chase down Russia's jet-powered Shahed drones, which can fly up to 500 km/h (310 mph). Trying to catch them at 100 mph won't cut it. Zelenskyy says some strikes have already gotten through because Ukraine's interceptors couldn't go fast enough.

The timing is... curious. US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner landed in Moscow today for talks with Putin. They're expected to meet Zelenskyy later at a summit in Ireland.

This isn't the first time Ukraine's Starshield access has been throttled. On October 14, Ukraine's military found that its speeds were being limited to around the same threshold. It happened as President Trump was publicly complaining about Ukraine's strikes on Russian refineries, which he blamed for high fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterms. Analysts agree that Trump's war with Iran has been the biggest factor in sky-high oil and diesel prices.

It still isn't clear who made the call to throttle Starshield the first time. Reuters, citing US officials, reported that the order came from inside the White House. But days later, the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk personally ordered the slowdown, possibly after talking with Trump. Trump denied any role, for whatever that's worth.

Musk's responses were all over the place. First, he said Starshield is "owned and controlled by the US government," then later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service. That's a strange thing to say about something you just said you don't own or control. Regardless, speeds were restored on October 24, reportedly after Trump called Musk and asked him to lift the caps amid bipartisan pressure from lawmakers.

Three weeks later, we're back where we started, with (so far) no one claiming responsibility.

#303. U.S. Forces in Europe Face Uncertainty as Pentagon Reviews Troop Levels

A U.S. Army unit in Germany is not sure what’s next as the Pentagon considers a major drawdown of forces in Europe.

When a specialized U.S. Army unit in Germany was told it might move to South Korea this fall, soldiers started packing their cutting-edge equipment. Months later, their boxed-up gear was still visible during a tour of the base, and the unit remains in the city of Wiesbaden, waiting.

The uncertainty comes as the Pentagon undertakes a six-month review of U.S. forces in Europe, weighing a potentially significant drawdown even as Russia wages war on NATO’s border.

The fate of the unit in Germany, which includes 1,000 people and is known as the Multi-Domain Command Europe, is a snapshot of the uncertainty that roughly 78,000 American troops stationed in Europe are facing before the results of the review are announced, which should take place by the end of the year.

A drawdown could cut into ports in Spain, air bases in England and Italy, and pockets of soldiers across Eastern Europe. Germany is particularly vulnerable, given that it hosts 37,000 U.S. troops — more than any other country in Europe.

Complicating the picture is the fact that President Trump has been using the military as a geopolitical tool, ordering the Pentagon to curtail military exercises with South Korea in part because it did not offer sufficient support for the Iran war. It remains unclear whether the Germany-based unit will in fact move to Asia, according to five U.S. officials and a former diplomat, all of whom spoke on the condition of anonymity to describe internal Pentagon discussions. Three of the officials said the unit began packing last summer.

A Pentagon spokesman said the review was continuing and that officials were working through a “predeployment notification process” for the unit in Germany.

#302. The Elder Scrolls' most obscure adventure gets a 4K PC makeover

N-Gage exclusive Shadowkey gets an AI-coded glow-up with plenty of new features.

So, you think you’re an Elder Scrolls superfan? You say you’ve been there since The Elder Scrolls Arena on MS-DOS and have logged hundreds of hours in The Elder Scrolls Online? You say you’ve bought all of the roughly 5,000 re-releases and remasters of Skyrim that have been made available in the last 15 years?

Tell me, then: What do you think of The Elder Scrolls Travels: Shadowkey?

What’s that? You’ve never played Shadowkey? You weren’t there day one back in 2004, Nokia N-Gage in hand, ready to experience the game’s 176 x 208 resolution mobile hack-and-slash RPG combat for yourself? And you call yourself a real Elder Scrolls fan?

Well, don’t worry. Now you can fill in this crucial gap in your history without even having to track down functional, decades-old N-Gage hardware. That’s thanks to Shadowkey: Reforged, a free fan-made PC port that pairs updated art with the original game files and scripts for an upscaled, 4K experience that runs natively on PC, no N-Gage or emulator needed.

Moving from a pre-iPhone mobile game to a modern PC obviously comes with major graphical upgrades, powered by a completely rewritten PC graphics engine. What was once a cramped portrait orientation on the N-Gage has now been expanded for a 16:9 monitor standard that displays at up to 120 fps (though the game’s logic still matches the original game’s 25 fps). In addition to newly retextured character and environmental models, the new port also sports small touches like bloom lighting, rippling water, and an upgraded interface with sharper text rendering than was possible on an ancient mobile phone.

Using the original game files (which you can obtain from an easy-to-find legitimate copy of the game, of course) means that Reforged‘s quests, dialogue, and game rules are “worked out from the original program,” as the game’s website puts it. That said, the game’s “Modern” mode adds a few touches like smarter enemy AI, fall damage, searchable containers, and a fast travel system that are missing from the original game (and from Reforged‘s more authentic “Classic” mode). The Modern mode also includes some welcome quality-of-life improvements like controller rumble, a smoother on-screen interface, and “better death poses.”

Those with a disposition against vibe-coded clones and ports should know that Reforged author Dan Whelan writes that he used “AI to assist with coding and building the engine, and with debugging,” as well as for the creation of “most of the modern, higher resolution artwork.” But those who are OK with this AI assistance should be happy to see the project moved from its first playable version in late October to a full-featured “release day” build for Windows, Mac, and Android that came out Wednesday. Whelan has been releasing daily updates to the project and seems set to continue, if the current “Version 0.2.9” designation is any indication.

The inherent limitations of 2000s-era N-Gage game development (which helped earn the original release some middling review scores) are still pretty apparent in even this Reforged remake. Still, it’s nice that Elder Scrolls completionists can now experience one of the series’ least-played releases. At the very least, it’s something to tinker with as we all continue the long wait for Elder Scrolls VI.

#299. Ars Live: What drone attacks on data centers mean for modern life in the cloud

A livestream Ars discussion is happening on November 17 at 1 pm Eastern Time.

Drone and missile strikes have inflicted unprecedented damage on data centers in Eastern Europe and the Middle East this year, disrupting cloud services for millions of people and permanently deleting some Amazon Web Services customer data. This trend represents a new concern for Big Tech companies—but also for governments and everyone else who depends on common digital services in modern life.

To explore what this all means for modern society’s reliance on cloud resilience, I will be hosting a live conversation titled “Drone attacks and data centers: New Risks for Big Tech” on Tuesday, November 17 at 1 pm ET with Joseph Jarnecki, a senior research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute (RUSI), a UK defense and security think tank. He leads RUSI’s work on how cloud services factor into national security and defense, along with tech sovereignty efforts.

Jarnecki has done extensive research on how Ukraine strengthened its cloud resilience with Western partners despite facing both cyberattacks and kinetic attacks at the start of the full-scale Russian invasion, and explored what that means for NATO and European governments’ cloud adoption plans.

This year, Jarnecki has written about Iran’s drone and missile strikes on Amazon data centers in the Middle East during the US-Israeli war with Iran, and the broader implications for governments and Big Tech. He has also commented on the impacts of Russia and Ukraine exchanging strikes on each other’s data centers.

For more background on the data center attacks that have transpired so far in 2026, you can see my previous Ars coverage of how the Iranian strikes left Amazon with months of repairs, and caused enough damage to result in the permanent loss of some AWS customer data.

I’ve also covered why Russia was not ready for the data center war it started with Ukraine, with the systematic Russian strike campaign targeting Ukraine’s data centers provoking Ukrainian counterstrikes that damaged Russian government data centers and AI data centers owned by the Russian tech giant Yandex.

My conversation with Jarnecki will explore why data centers have become much more prominent military targets, whether defending or hardening data centers against drones and missiles is a realistic option, how cloud resiliency can be improved in wartime conditions, and the choices governments and cloud providers face in deploying data centers during an era of growing geopolitical risks.

You can watch the discussion live on YouTube on November 17 at 1 pm ET. We will also be fielding a few audience questions.

#296. Australia Unveils New A.I. Regulator After OpenAI Breach

The Australian government will establish a new artificial intelligence safety watchdog that will enforce “significant penalties” for companies that fail to adhere to mandatory safeguards, Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said on Tuesday.

The government also announced sweeping recommendations to prevent future breaches after OpenAI models went rogue and gained unauthorized access to government systems this year, including considering criminal law changes to penalize companies that develop “an AI model that engages in harmful conduct, and the company fails to take reasonable steps to prevent that harm.”

The breach, which occurred in June but was not reported to Australian officials until three months later, raised doubts about whether laws in Australia, or elsewhere, are ready for the challenges posed by the fast-evolving technology.

“Our task is to make sure that we shape A.I. rather than A.I. shape us,” Mr. Albanese said on Tuesday.

“This incident was completely unacceptable,” he added. “And it can’t be allowed to happen again.”

(The New York Times has sued OpenAI and Microsoft, claiming copyright infringement of news content related to A.I. systems. The two companies have denied those claims.)

The breach occurred on June 18 by agents operating under an OpenAI research team that was carrying out research into public medicine spending using an internal model, on a data portal for Australia’s public health insurance system, Medicare.

#294. Who is Collette Kress, NVIDIA's interim CEO?

Kress spent 25 years in various finance roles before joining NVIDIA in 2013

After 33 years as the chief executive officer of NVIDIA, Jensen Huang has resigned from the AI and gaming giant he founded. On Thursday morning, the company's board of director announced Colette Kress, NVIDIA's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as Huang's temporary replacement.

"The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," newly-minted NVIDIA board chair Harvey Jones said in a release announcing Thursday's news.

Kress is a relative unknown, but is taking lead of NVIDIA at tumultuous time in the company's history. Huang departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him. Before the revelations, the company did not have a succession plan in place. And for at least the immediate future, it's now on Kress to lead the company. Here's what we know about the executive.

Kress worked at three companies before joining NVIDIA. She first spent eight years in finance roles at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. In 2006, six years after Steve Ballmer took over as Microsoft's CEO, she became the chief financial officer of the company's Server and Tools division. She remained with the company until 2010, when she moved to Cisco. There, she was the senior vice president and chief financial officer of the company's Business Technology and Operations Finance unit.

Per a 2023 interview, Huang wanted to transform NVIDIA into the dominant player in the nascent AI industry. Say what you will about Huang's personal life and judgement, the man was an AI visionary.

Kress partly credits her sons, who were only eight and 10 at the time, for buying Huang's pitch. Despite their young age, the two already knew of NVIDIA and its importance to gaming. In a 2024 Fortune interview, Kress said NVIDIA was the smallest company she had worked for when she joined in 2013.

At the start of her tenure, NVIDIA's revenue was $4.28 billion for the year. In fiscal 2026, the company recorded a record $215.9 billion in revenue.

When NVIDIA's board of directors announced Kress as Huang's temporary successor, it said it would consider both internal and external candidates to take on the role of CEO permanently, leaving the door open for her and other current NVIDIA employees to win the job. In any case, it's far too early to speculate who the company's board might interview for the job. But if you can forgive a writer a tired analogy, if Kress stays on as CEO, she is likely to be a Tim Cook-like figure, one that can stabilize the company and guide it toward continued growth.

"The Board, [executive staff] and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication," Kress wrote in her first email to NVIDIA employees after the transition was announced, according to CNBC.

Still, she has a very difficult job ahead of her. Huang has agreed to testify before Congress in a hearing that the House aims to hold before the Thanksgiving holiday. The chair of the House Foreign Affairs committee, Brian Mast of Florida, said he wasn't ruling out the possibility of "public hearings for other people or other things down road." You can bet NVIDIA is likely to face an intense amount of scrutiny in the coming weeks, particularly when Democrats take control of the House and Senate at the start of next year.

#293. FAA expands AI advice to manage air traffic congestion across entire US airspace

Airlines praised the AI tool, but the air traffic controllers’ union wasn’t involved.

The US government has expanded the use of an AI tool for advising air traffic controllers to assist in managing air traffic across the entire national airspace system. Airline executives have voiced support for the rollout, but the US labor union representing air traffic controllers has said it was not involved in designing or testing the AI-driven system.

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) says the $875 million SMART system has helped reroute 180 flights since being implemented in airspace over Washington, DC, on September 21. The agency claims that this saved an average of 10 minutes per flight and almost 80 gallons of fuel per flight—and it is apparently hoping such results can be replicated nationwide through the expanded deployment.

“After a successful initial rollout to the National Capital Region in September, the FAA will now use SMART in the airspace of all 22 centers across the country to reduce delays, reduce fuel consumption and improve the travel experience for the flying public,” according to the agency statement posted on November 10.

The FAA fact sheet describes SMART as a cloud-based system that combines information about “weather patterns, flight paths, traffic flow and controller staffing metrics” to predict air traffic patterns. It is designed to make scheduling recommendations, identify optimal flight routes, and determine the best order of arrivals for aircraft.

However, a human team of FAA specialists based in DC is ultimately responsible for validating the SMART system recommendations before passing them along to air traffic control facilities across the country, according to the agency.

Furthermore, the AI-powered SMART system “cannot be used to replace the critical role air traffic controllers play or take over control of an aircraft,” the FAA clarified in the September 21 announcement about the initial deployment. Local air traffic controllers can decide whether or not to accept the SMART system recommendations.

The previous FAA announcement included supportive statements from Delta Air Lines CEO Ed Bastian and American Airlines CEO Robert Isom.

But the National Air Traffic Controllers Association, which represents more than 15,000 members, also told Flying magazine that it was not consulted or involved in the “design, testing, or implementation of SMART and therefore cannot speak to its effect on the air traffic control workforce.”

Following the initial deployment in airspace above DC, the FAA expanded the SMART system’s coverage to air route traffic control centers in Atlanta along with Jacksonville and Miami in Florida, according to The Dallas Morning News.

The FAA also shared an example with The Dallas Morning News about how SMART recommended a shorter and faster route for a cargo flight arriving at Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport, although the newspaper noted that the agency did not identify the flight involved and has not provided information about how SMART may have assisted passenger flight traffic.

Ars has previously covered the SMART system’s development and lingering questions about how it works, as well as the air traffic controller shortage and other challenges facing the FAA.

#290. In reversal, American Cancer Society urges prostate cancer tests for all men

Men aged 50–69 and at average risk should get PSA screening every two years.

In a significant update to its guidelines for prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society is now recommending that all men get routinely screened for prostate cancer with a PSA test.

Men at average risk should get a PSA test every two years from age 50 to 69, according to the new guidelines released Thursday.

Men considered at high risk due to race and family history should start testing earlier, at 45, and most are recommended to get tested every year. Specifically, yearly testing starting at age 45 is recommended for all Black men, who have higher rates of prostate cancer. Non-Black men should also get yearly testing starting at 45 if a close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer before age 65, or if more than one of their relatives has had prostate cancer. On the other hand, if only one close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer and it was diagnosed at age 65 or older, non-Black men in this case should start PSA screening at 45, but are recommended to get testing only every two years.

Men ages 70 to 74 should consult their doctor on continuing screening, while those 75 and up are not recommended to get screened.

The new guidance departs from ACS’s previous iteration, published in 2010, which made no universal recommendations for routine screening. Men at age 50 were recommended to use shared-decision making with their doctors on whether or not to get tested. The recommendation was similar to that from the US Preventive Services Task Force, a federal panel that regularly evaluates evidence and makes recommendations on such preventive medical care. (The USPSTF is currently working on an update to its prostate cancer screening recommendation, though it should be noted that the panel has recently been overhauled by anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and new members are reportedly rife with conflicts of interest.)

In announcing the guidelines today, the ACS says its updates are based on new evidence since 2010 that shows screening saves lives, and that advances in case management can avoid the concerning harms from overdiagnosis and overtreatment. For many men with prostate cancer, their cancer will never grow or cause harm in their lifetime, while treatment can run the risk of erectile dysfunction and urinary incontinence.

In the new guidance, the ACS adds guardrails, firstly advising all men to be informed about the risks and benefits. If a PSA test comes back high—defined as 3 ng/mL or higher for men under 70 and 6.5 ng/mL or higher for those 70 and up—the ACS recommends repeating the test before taking any next steps.

If repeat testing is still high, then men should get either a prostate MRI or a validated biomarker test to assess risk. Only if there is a high suspicion of cancer—such as having a very high PSA test result—should men jump to a prostate biopsy.

For men diagnosed with prostate cancer, active surveillance should be the initial management strategy for men whose cancer appears to be at low risk of growing or spreading.

“By catching aggressive cancers with a simple PSA blood test, you open the door to highly effective treatments that protect your future,” Dr. Shanthi Sivendran, interim chief patient officer for the ACS, said in a statement. “Meanwhile, lower-risk findings can be safely managed through careful, active surveillance.”

Prostate cancer is one of the most common cancers in men and the second leading cause of cancer death in US men, the ACS notes. Nearly 334,00 men are estimated to be diagnosed with prostate cancer in 2026, and it’s estimated that over 36,320 will die from the disease.

#288. The Jensen Huang scandal is hardly over

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A second affair has hit the news cycle, but that’s not what matters here.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A second affair has hit the news cycle, but that’s not what matters here.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I was certain, until the story of Jensen Huang’s second affair hit, that Huang was bulletproof. After all, why wouldn’t he be? He’s placed himself indispensably at the center of the AI industry and famously had no succession plan at Nvidia. In fact, he’s the only CEO Nvidia has ever had. L’état c’est moi.

Then two stories were published nearly back to back on Wednesday: one alleging Huang had an affair with his subordinate Kimberly Powell, another that the board was meeting to consider his ouster. First thing Thursday morning, Nvidia announced Huang had resigned. One of the most powerful men in Silicon Valley was out.

Why throw Huang under the bus for getting his nuts drained?

Fascinating. First of all, when I asked on Monday “Who else is Jensen Huang fucking?” I did not expect to get answers this fast — somebody, potentially somebody on the board (as we’ve seen in other incidents), wanted the story out there. Second, why throw Huang under the bus for getting his nuts drained? This is not an industry that cares about that kind of thing — just ask Eric Schmidt and Bill Gates. My guess is that the second affair is a pretext for action, and this all goes back to the original story from last week: that Huang was honeypotted by an alleged Chinese spy. Whatever was going on with neocloud Megaspeed might be much, much worse than we know, and what we know is pretty bad: business listings that led to “a Malaysian data center and shopping mall, a near-empty office in Singapore and a dilapidated storefront outside Kuala Lumpur.”

Do you have information about Nvidia? Contact Elizabeth Lopatto securely on Signal at lopatto.46.

Even months before the report that Alice “No Relation” Huang, an exec at Megaspeed, was having an affair with Huang, there were questions about Nvidia’s business in China. The US government has placed strict export controls on Nvidia’s high-end chips. Huang claimed in May that as a result, Nvidia has zero percent market share in China. But also in May, short sellers Culper Research published a report in which they wrote, “We believe that in reality, over 20% of Nvidia’s FY 2026 compute revenues remained driven by China – supported both by illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries.”

Megaspeed was Nvidia’s largest Southeast Asian buyer, but Singaporean newspaper The Straits Times visited Megaspeed’s Singapore office a month ago to find “no signs of activity.” Another building tenant told the reporters, “It is always dark for as long as it has been here. I hardly see anyone visiting or in the office.” The sole director of Megaspeed, Tan Yong Pong, appears to have vanished. The Singaporean company that owns Megaspeed, Swiftdata, has listed an office that belongs to an interior design firm as its business address.

Incidentally, Alice Huang has surfaced in Beijing. The Financial Times says she’s barred from Singapore for giving police “inaccurate information.” Oh, and reportedly her companies invested in Chinese AI company DeepSeek and promised them Nvidia’s Blackwell chips? Fun!

Megaspeed is only part of the story, I think. There are two Chinese chip-related scandals that could cause serious problems for Nvidia, and both feature people with close ties to Huang. One is Megaspeed, which involves Huang’s maybe-spy affair partner. The other centers on Super Micro (which, horribly, does business as Supermicro), a partner of Nvidia run by one of Huang’s old friends, Charles Liang. Super Micro’s cofounder, Yih-Shyan “Wally” Liaw, along with Ruei-Tsang “Steven” Chang and Ting-Wei “Willy” Sun, allegedly defrauded the US by serving as a passthrough for a front company to buy $2.5 billion of Nvidia chips for Alibaba. The front company, not named in the indictment, is Thailand-based OBON, according to Bloomberg. Liang and Super Micro have not been charged.

But these incidents may not be the end of Nvidia’s problems. Take for instance, more recently, New Matrix — another company that barely exists, and yet is an approved buyer of Nvidia chips. It is, in fact, one of several approved companies that “have ties to firms that have been blacklisted by the U.S. government, or that work as military contractors in China,” according to The New York Times.

The question, of course, is how high this all goes

Huang has insisted that Nvidia has seen “no evidence of any AI chip diversion.” He’s been pretty emphatic on that point, actually. I am now wondering if that is even a little bit true. If the board is cutting Huang loose, there’s probably more on chip smuggling allegations coming.

More substantively, Nvidia reportedly intervened in a 2025 Commerce Department investigation of Megaspeed, which was suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips — halting the investigation. Since the scandal broke, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick appears to have become the fall guy for this, with Axios reporting that Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent “confronted” him about failing to note Megaspeed as a national security concern and Politico saying “more than half a dozen Republicans have demanded answers” from him.

The question, of course, is how high this all goes. After all, Huang was awfully buddy-buddy with President Donald Trump, who was informed about Huang’s maybe-spy girlfriend in 2025 and apparently did nothing about it. Incidentally, Nvidia has reportedly donated $25 million to Trump’s planned ballroom. This is what gangster tech regulation looks like.

I wonder what else was an open secret at the company, you know?

Come January, Elizabeth Warren will be able to send subpoenas in her role as the chair of the Senate Banking Committee. And wouldn’t you know, she seems to be pretty interested. Democrats will control both the House and the Senate. Their voters have given them a mandate to root out corruption. And boy howdy, does Nvidia look like a sitting duck right now. If Huang knew chips were being diverted — or had reason to know that — and tried to ensure their sales anyway, he may have committed actual crimes (bribery, espionage, smuggling, whatever) and, under a functioning legal system, could actually go to jail. Nvidia dispensing with its indispensable CEO is the company reducing its risk of getting roped into Huang’s drama.

Will it be that easy for Nvidia? I wonder. The most interesting part of the second affair story, with Powell, the Nvidia vice president of healthcare, comes at the very end of the New York Times article. When someone took a video of Powell and Huang being “affectionate” in March, Huang’s security had nondisclosure agreements ready to go. CNBC reports that rumors of their affair have circulated “for years.” I wonder what else was an open secret at the company, you know? Especially since Huang had 60 direct reports, so every part of the company is covered in his fingerprints.

Over in the tech world, people still seem to be in denial. On X, the social network for Trumpist dead-enders, the account “zerohedge” posted, “Elon please hire Jensen.” Elon Musk replied, “That would be an honor.”

#284. Shania Twain calls for equal pay and more diversity in country music

Shania Twain is standing up for others in country music.

The legendary star in the genre used her acceptance speech for the Equal Play Award at Sunday’s CMT Awards to call for more equity and diversity in the country music industry.

“When I wrote the phrase ‘Man I feel like a woman’ I honestly had no idea at the time it would become an undercurrent of a decades-long career,” she said, referring to one of her biggest hits. “It’s amazing how one statement can empower so many.”

The phrase, Twain said, “has been adopted by an array of fantastic communities all around the world and become a genuine path of power…which brings me so much joy.”

“I believe in an all-inclusive country music. We’re family,” Twain said. “This is a genre of music with a rich history that raised and nurtured my own songwriting and performance and recording career from childhood. Currently, the industry standard does a real disservice to this.”

Twain, who was presented the award by rapper Megan Thee Stallion, shouted out some of the diverse acts she is spotlighting on her “Queen of Me” tour this year, including Lily Rose, Lindsay Ell, Hailey Whitters, Mickey Guyton and others.

“I will continue to do my best as a trailblazer,” Twain said. “Together, let’s ensure all our fellow artists get equal play regardless of gender, age or race.”

She also performed acappella with the African American singing group, The BoykinZ, while presenting an award onstage. Twain connected with the group, who are sisters and rising country stars, after Kelly Clarkson surprised them recently by bringing Twain out to meet them during an episode of Clarkson’s daytime talk show.

#283. David Walliams, UK Celebrity, Is Accused of Sexual Misconduct

The Guardian and ITV News published claims from multiple women against the comedian and writer, who fought a weekslong court battle to keep the claims private. He denies the accusations.

David Walliams, a British TV personality and best-selling children’s author, was publicly accused on Tuesday of exploiting his celebrity status to prey on women and teenagers as young as 15 after a weekslong court battle to keep the accusations private.

The allegations emerged after a judge decided on Nov. 3 that The Guardian newspaper and ITV News, a British broadcaster, could publish the claims against Mr. Walliams.

Mr. Walliams denies the allegations. In a letter sent to news outlets, he said he accepted he had “behaved thoughtlessly towards other people I had casual sexual relationships with,” but he added: “I am not a sexual predator. I have not broken the law.”

The news organizations had been held back from publishing their joint investigation because of a temporary injunction, an English legal measure that a judge can impose to bar the identification of an individual. The secrecy had driven a swirl of online speculation in recent weeks about the identity of the unnamed celebrity.

Mr. Walliams is a household name in the United Kingdom who rose to fame in the 2000s as a star of “Little Britain,” a popular BBC sketch show that poked fun at British archetypes, and he later became best known for his children’s novels. In recent years, he has been dogged by accusations over his professional conduct, including reports that he had made derogatory and sexually explicit remarks about contestants during the recording of an episode of the television show “Britain’s Got Talent,” for which he was a judge.

The investigation that The Guardian and ITV News published on Tuesday, which the two organizations had worked on since April 2023, was based on accounts from 30 people who accused Mr. Walliams of abusing his power to sexually prey on women and targeting teenagers, some of whom met him when they were as young as 15.

#282. 2023 March Madness: Will UConn or San Diego State triumph in the national championship game that 'means everything?'

The University of Connecticut won its fifth men’s basketball national title with a 76-59 victory over San Diego State University on Monday night at NRG Stadium in Houston.

Senior guard Tristen Newton led UConn (31-8) with 19 points and 10 rebounds while Final Four Most Outstanding Player Adama Sanogo, a junior forward, chipped in with 17 points and 10 rebounds.

“We weren’t ranked going into the year so we had the chip on our shoulder,” UConn head coach Dan Hurley told game broadcaster CBS. “We knew the level that we could play at, even through those dark times,” he added, referencing the team’s six losses in eight games during the regular season.

He said going into the tournament his group had confidence garnered during the season.

“And when you have the type of leaders like Andre Jackson (game-high six assists Monday) and Adama Sanogo, they kept this team together, got us back on track and we knew we were the best team in the tournament going in and we just had to play to our level,” he added.

San Diego State (32-7) was topped by Keshad Johnson who had 14 points.

UConn trailed very early but San Diego State was undone by an 11-minute, eight-second stretch in which they scored just five free throws and missed 12 consecutive shots from the field. The Huskies went from down 10-6 to up 36-24 at halftime.

The Aztecs made a run midway through the second half and narrowed the deficit to five at 60-55 with 5:19 to play but the Huskies scored the next nine to take a comfortable lead into the final two minutes.

“We battled. Battled back to five in the second half, but gave them too much separation,” San Diego State coach Brian Dutcher said. “We had to be at our best. We weren’t at our best. A lot had to do with UConn.”

Senior guard Adam Seiko told reporters they gave themselves a chance with their second half comeback but UConn “just made a little bit more plays” at the end.

“They have a lot of weapons. They were pretty good,” said Matt Bradley, also a senior guard. “To beat them, we had to make shots. I shot poorly. And you had to have a really good game to beat those dudes on the offensive end.”

UConn won each of its six tournament games by at least 10 points, with its closest game being a 13-point win over the University of Miami in the national semifinals.

“I just want to thank my teammates, my coaches who believed in me. If it were not for them I would not be here right now,” Sanogo told CBS.

Jordan Hawkins, who scored 16 points for UConn, talked about winning the crown one day after his cousin, Angel Reese of Louisiana State University, won the women’s title.

“I mean it’s absolutely amazing that we both get this opportunity and I mean the family reunion is going to be great so that’s all I know,” he said.

UConn enters rarefied air as only the sixth team to win five NCAA men’s basketball championships, joining UCLA (11), Kentucky (eight), North Carolina (six), Duke (five) and Indiana (five). All of UConn’s titles have come since 1999 with the most recent before Monday occurring in 2014.

UConn’s women’s teams have won 11 basketball national titles.

CNN’s Steve Almasy and Issy Ronald contributed to this report.

#280. No, AI agents did not discover a previously unknown Maya site

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Scientists are getting tired of amateurs parachuting into their fields and making ‘discoveries’ with AI.﻿

Scientists are getting tired of amateurs parachuting into their fields and making ‘discoveries’ with AI.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Armed with a team of AI agents, Google researcher Pietro Miotti claimed earlier this week to have discovered a potentially unmapped Maya site in Mexico. There was just one problem: The site had already been discovered and was published in an academic journal last year. The incident, which sparked criticism among archeologists online, underscores a growing tension in science as amateurs wielding increasingly powerful AI tools parachute into specialist fields, often without the expertise needed to understand what they are looking at.

Local communities are often already well aware of sites in their area.

Miotti said the idea to use AI to search for archeological sites came to him while traveling in Peru, when he began wondering what historic explorers might do with access to modern technology. He settled on lidar, a laser-based mapping technology that can produce detailed three-dimensional images of the environment, and set a team of 48 AI agents to comb through NASA lidar data for signs of undocumented sites. As “Peru isn’t well covered” by public lidar data, Miotti wrote, he shifted his focus to the Maya region.

The agents flagged numerous promising locations, including “one major village [that] doesn’t seem to have been registered” in existing records. Miotti, who described the research to The Verge as a personal side project done without any Google compute or resources, said he then reached out to Mexico’s National Institute of Anthropology and History (INAH) to check whether the apparent discovery was already known, including through unpublished research. Using the agents to translate his correspondence into Spanish, Miotti said INAH told him it had no record of a registered site at that location.

“It took less than 72 hours to go from a random thought while traveling to an official reply from the Mexican government. This is by far the best time ever to be alive,” Miotti wrote on his blog.

Miotti stressed that he is not an archeologist and emphasized that the finding would need to be verified with fieldwork. Nevertheless, he hailed it as proof that “we’re on the brink of a revolution across every science” and called for the “entire planet” to be mapped with lidar so AI agents could “help us find the rest.” Had he been an archaeologist familiar with the region, however, Miotti might have spotted a problem: The site was already known, and had been documented in an academic journal more than a year earlier.

“I didn’t expect it to get this much attention.”

Archeologists were quick to point out the oversight on social media. In a series of posts on Bluesky, Sarah Parcak, an archeology professor at the University of Alabama at Birmingham, stressed the importance of carefully checking findings against existing research. “This is why we have peer review,” Parcak wrote, adding that just because the Mexican government doesn’t have something in its database doesn’t mean it is unknown or unmapped. Other archeologists echoed her criticism, slamming the project for spreading misinformation and taking credit away from archeologists, who have been using advanced digital tools in their work for decades.

“This was a personal project that I was excited to share,” Miotti told The Verge. “I didn’t expect it to get this much attention.” He added that as a non-archaeologist, he had not realized a site not being registered wasn’t the same as it not having been mapped, adding that expert knowledge “is needed to verify what the agents find.” He also addressed the limits of his AI tools: “My agents and I missed it. Archaeologists rightly don’t publish the coordinates of sites, so checking whether a site is already known means comparing images against figures in papers, not searching a database. That’s much harder for agents, and it’s something these tools need to get better at. When people pointed it out, I updated the post and the blog as soon as I could to credit their work.”

Nevertheless, Miotti said this was an example of how agents could be used to help experts sort through large amounts of data. He said he has been encouraged by the archaeologists and PhD students that he said were reaching out to him “excited about the workflow and wanting to know how it works and whether it could help with their own survey data.”

“Our ‘problem’ is not discovering new sites,” Elizabeth Graham, a professor of Mesoamerican archaeology at University College London, told The Verge. “Most of us working in the Maya area know about tons of sites not explored or excavated or mapped, most of these found on foot,” she said. Graham also critiqued the use of the word “discover,” noting that local communities are often already well aware of sites in their area.

While she credited Miotti with doing a decent job of using AI to map the mounds from lidar data — “the interpretation of lidar data can be tricky” — Graham argued the bigger obstacle is funding for research. “The fact is, there just isn’t the funding to excavate or map sites as there was in the past, so in a way it doesn’t matter how many more are ‘discovered.’”

“Just spotting something does not add much to our knowledge.”

The controversy reflects a broader problem with AI in science, perhaps most visible with OpenAI’s recent advances in mathematics. While AI tools are increasingly capable of producing genuinely impressive results, they don’t always engage with the existing research those findings build upon. The result can be claimed breakthroughs that have, as is the case here, already been made by someone else, or a general failure to credit earlier work. Even when the findings are actually new, they can lack context a specialist would bring. Ultimately, amateur use of AI tools often ends up shifting the burden of establishing what has actually been found and why it matters to actual experts, work that would ordinarily be part of making a scientific discovery in the first place.

Ivan Šprajc, a professor of archaeology at the Research Centre of the Slovenian Academy of Sciences and Arts in Ljubljana and a coauthor of the paper documenting the site, told The Verge he wasn’t particularly impressed by the agents’ work. Archeologists have been using lidar and “other sophisticated remote sensing tools for quite some time,” he said, adding that “just spotting something does not add much to our knowledge.” He told The Verge that while it is commendable that Miotti checked in with INAH, doing so is “not sufficient” as the institution does not have access to all possible information. “The fact that INAH has no official knowledge of a particular cluster of archaeological features does not necessarily mean that it has not been spotted or even explored by archaeologists.”

Šprajc said that he worries the growing use of AI tools by amateurs could overwhelm archeologists with claims of new discoveries. “Just today I have received an email from another AI ‘discoverer,’ so I am a little bit concerned about being flooded by this kind of message,” he said. He also raised another potential risk of amateurs publicizing the locations of archeological sites: attracting looters. “But as long as these findings do not lead to looting, I don’t see a major problem,” he added.

#278. Who Is David Walliams, the UK Entertainer Accused of Sexual Misconduct?

The British entertainer rose to fame with the television show “Little Britain” and has published over 40 children’s books.

The British entertainer David Walliams, who was publicly accused on Tuesday of a pattern of sexual misconduct, is a comedian and prolific author of children’s books who has been a fixture on Britain’s television screens for more than 20 years.

Mr. Walliams, 55, rose to fame as a sketch show comedian in the 2000s and later as a judge on “Britain’s Got Talent.” He is also one of the country’s most successful children’s book authors, publishing over 40 titles that have sold tens of millions of copies.

On Tuesday, two British news organizations, The Guardian and ITV News, published detailed allegations that Mr. Walliams had exploited his celebrity status to sexually prey on women, including some he had met when they were as young as 15. Mr. Walliams, who fought a weekslong court battle to keep the claims private, has denied the accusations. “I am not a sexual predator,” he wrote in a letter sent to news outlets.

Mr. Walliams burst onto the national stage in Britain in the early 2000s as a transgressive comedy writer and entertainer.

In his breakout BBC comedy sketch show “Little Britain,” which first aired in 2003, he and his comedy partner Matt Lucas played a series of eccentric characters who poked fun at almost every minority group imaginable, including mothers on welfare benefits, wheelchair users and gay men.

The show pushed the boundaries of acceptability, even during a period of provocative comedy television in Britain. Mr. Walliams and Mr. Lucas donned fat suits, caked themselves in makeup and wore blackface.

#277. Meet the people who want peptides to be made in America

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For some, the answer to a dodgy gray market is to bring the pharmaceutical supply chain back to American soil.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

This is Optimizer, a weekly newsletter sent from Verge senior reviewer Victoria Song that dissects and discusses the latest gizmos and potions that swear they’re going to change your life. We’ll be taking a break next week and will be back on November 25th. Opt in for Optimizer here.

The SS Jeremiah O’Brien is a World War II Liberty ship. The hulking vessel was built in 56 days. It made 11 trips to Normandy, France, to supply the Allied powers in the fight against German U-boats. Last week, it was the site of a black-tie peptide party thrown by a trio of Gen Z venture capitalists.

Roughly 150 people attended the event, where four types of peptide samples were provided to guests — no injections. One was a topical cream with GHK-Cu, while the other three were dissolvable sublingual strips from peptide startup Afterglow. GHK-Cu is a popular skincare ingredient, but it’s unclear how well sublingual peptide strips work. The topic of the night wasn’t just the state of peptides. The venue was a deliberate reference to American industrial prowess — and why the pharmaceutical supply chain ought to be centered in the United States.

The timing couldn’t have been more pointed.

Sourcing is one of the biggest question marks with gray-market peptides — something the US Department of Health and Human Services is trying to address with its recent interim policy decision allowing certain unapproved peptides for compounding.

The decision is a controversial one. FDA career scientists haven’t endorsed it, though to the average person it might seem like they have. In fact, at a public hearing in July, FDA scientists categorically stated that there wasn’t enough evidence proving safety or efficacy for the experimental peptides in question. Some industry experts have cautioned that the HHS’s decision threatens the current clinical trial framework. By circumventing gold-standard clinical trials, it arguably allows telehealth providers and compounding pharmacies to bypass the approval process and create a parallel, dubiously regulated market.

But for the party’s organizers Roxana Grunenwald, Yuva Krishnapillai, and Elia Ahmadi — all three are from Haus Fund, a biotech accelerator — this was an opportunity to make the case for American peptides. Hence the party on a wartime cargo ship, where guests left with stickers and hats emblazoned with “My Peptides Are Made in America.”

Listening to the three speak, they wouldn’t strike you as typical hooah patriots or MAHA activists — though there’s some overlap in their talking points. They’re venture capitalists promoting a certain viewpoint that’s trending in Silicon Valley right now. But it’s not just them making this argument. Hims & Hers — one of the most recognizable telehealth brands right now — is also hopping on the American peptide bandwagon.

The push for American manufacturing is a thorny one. Last year, President Donald Trump’s tariff policies also reignited the American manufacturing argument, though the reality is it’s difficult to return to “made in America” after decades spent shaping the country into a services-based economy.

Pharmaceuticals have also globalized. According to the US Pharmacopeia, 51 percent of all US prescription active pharmaceutical ingredients (API) comes from India and the European Union. China accounts for 10 percent. But when it comes to gray-market peptides, there’s a reason they’re commonly referred to as “Chinese peptides.” The vast majority of these research chemicals sold online come from Chinese manufacturers.

“Right now, Americans are buying consumer peptides from gray-market sellers in China. And that concerns me because American consumers have little recourse if something goes wrong,” Ahmadi said in response to a panel question. “This is not a blanket criticism of China. This is not a criticism of Chinese people. America needs to be able to make its own medicines.”

“I think this is a really good first step in solving the gray market problem,” Krishnapillai tells me, referring to the HHS’s interim decision. While building industrial infrastructure is a lengthy, complicated, and expensive process, he argues that this decision opens the door for compounding pharmacies to build a supply chain that adheres to US consumer safety laws. That in turn could give the FDA more insight into how to more effectively regulate the market. “The FDA [will be] able to see from a bird’s-eye view a lot more clarity, which will allow for structure to be built out and eventually a new path to API classification,” he adds.

“We’re taking the stance that if people will be purchasing these peptides and taking them, sometimes even injecting, we want them to be American peptides,” adds Grunenwald. “We want them to be both compounded, produced, and verified on American soil.”

“The hardware industry — semiconductors, the compute industry, electronics — have all recognized that the reliance on China for critical minerals is a huge issue. That’s very clear. The bio industry has not yet had that same pushback and the same epiphany about our reliance on Chinese APIs. We aim to bring this onshore. It’ll take a lot of money and a lot of time, but it’s not impossible,” says Grunenwald.

For Ahmadi, onshoring peptides back to America is also a matter of regulatory consistency.

“That made-in-America stamp is really only the beginning,” he says. “If we don’t have our own domestic production, we won’t be able to enforce our own regulations. Our markets are always going to be flooded by foreign imports and we won’t be able to have accountability to American consumers. So, we see this as the first step in a larger conversation to have America set the global standard for quality and safety in this new space.”

Broadly speaking, these points have some merit. Several gray-market peptide users I’ve spoken with for an upcoming story stated that the mere act of importing “research drugs” and worrying about customs was a significant source of anxiety. That said, cost is another major reason why my sources tell me they opted to seek Chinese suppliers. It’s too soon to tell, but American-made peptides will likely be more expensive. That’s because an end-to-end US supply chain for these drugs is no easy feat.

Even so, for some, that extra expense is worth it if it means signaling a higher-quality product.

“We purchased an API manufacturing facility in California recently … Our intention is to leverage that capability to provide US-made API, and as a result, US-made product through the whole supply chain,” says Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, chief medical officer at Hims & Hers. The company purchased the facility last year, though it does partner with other companies globally to deliver compounded medications outside the US.

But in this particular case, Vinjamoori is also specifically referring to Peptides Plus, a forthcoming service from Hims & Hers announced in the wake of the HHS interim policy. In a blog announcing Peptides Plus, Hims & Hers CEO Andrew Dudum writes that the company “spent years building the infrastructure we need in our very own U.S.-based peptide manufacturing facility.” At launch, the company plans to provide access to “Sermorelin, Glutathione, B12, NAD+, and L-carnitine, with BPC-157 and TB-500 to follow when allowable.” Notably, some of these substances, like vitamin B12 and NAD+, aren’t peptides at all. Meanwhile, sermorelin and glutathione are already legal. All of these, with the exception of BPC-157 and TB-500, are currently available through legal pathways.

“I think it’s important not just to be able to say, ‘Oh, this is US made,’ but because it gives us direct oversight into the whole process,” says Vinjamoori, who says that he had prescribed peptides to patients in the past before the Biden administration moved certain research peptides to the no-compounding list.

“Even then, there was inconsistency between compounding pharmacies. Everyone knew this,” he says, noting that doctors would individually recommend pharmacies based on what they were prescribing. “That to me, is still not ideal. That’s why we really felt it was worth making this investment — and to be clear, this is not easy.”

When asked about how this might affect Peptide Plus pricing, Vinjamoori demurred, stating that the company would have more to share in “due time.”

There are yet many unknowns with regard to the HHS decision and how it’ll impact the peptide economy at large. The decision is most notably an interim policy. It’s not necessarily a permanent or lasting change. It was also made before the midterm elections, so who can say how the policy winds will shift concerning tariffs and whether the zeal for “made in America” will fizzle as a result.

But for now, it’s more than likely that you’ll start seeing some vendors touting that their peptides are proudly made in America. It’s entirely possible that they might imply that makes their product “safer.” That might be true with regard to sourcing and, to a degree, accountability. But it’s not the whole story. After all, in peptide America, sourcing is only part of the problem.

#273. U.S. Received $12 Billion From Venezuela’s Oil, Report Shows

The workings of a U.S.-controlled bank account shed light on how Washington manages Venezuelan revenue after capturing the country’s president.

The U.S. government received more than $12 billion in revenue from Venezuela’s natural resource exports in the first nine months of this year, according to a confidential report by the global financial services firm KPMG.

The vast majority of the funds were distributed back to Venezuela soon after they were received, the report shows. It is unclear if the money stayed in the country or who the ultimate recipients were.

The report, provides the most detailed portrait so far of the financial control exerted by the United States government on Venezuela since U.S. forces toppled the country’s president in January. The report was reviewed by The New York Times and its contents have not been previously reported.

The Trump administration installed a pliant interim government and charged it with opening up Venezuela’s oil industry to American investment.

The report was written just days before the midterm elections and Democratic lawmakers had repeatedly vowed to bring more transparency and accountability to Washington’s policy toward Venezuela.

Now that Democrats have won control of Congress in the midterm elections, several aides to Democratic lawmakers said they would continue pressing the Trump administration to describe what it was doing to prevent corruption in Venezuela after taking control of the country’s finances.

#272. Microsoft Outlook is down

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft 365 issues are cutting people off from their email right now.﻿

Microsoft 365 issues are cutting people off from their email right now.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Over the last hour or so, reports have come in from around the world that people can’t access their Outlook email, either through the web or apps. Microsoft has acknowledged the issue from its Microsoft 365 Status account, saying that “We’ve determined a portion of infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors.”

There were also some reports of problems with Teams, but some of the people reporting issues say Teams is still working now.

While we haven’t identified any recent changes that correlate with the start of impact, our investigation indicates the failed health checks may originate from a specific component on the affected servers that’s responsible for processing them.

We’re deploying a targeted change to bypass this component on a subset of servers to validate whether this restores availability, and we’ve taken steps to prevent additional servers from being removed from service while we work to return the affected servers to service. We’re also investigating why affected servers are experiencing elevated processor usage while handling requests.

But it’s still unclear how long it will take before access is restored, or if that will fix all of the issues people are seeing. On DownDetector, reports of issues with Outlook started to spike just before 1PM ET, and they have not completely gone away yet. It’s also probably not the best sign that we’re struggling to consistently load Microsoft’s own status pages to check on progress. If there are any more details, we will update this article.

Update, 3:42PM ET: Added latest status update from Microsoft.

#271. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.
